## Hartshorne, *Algebraic Geometry* — Chapter I: Varieties

| ID              | Kind / source unit                                                                          | Location                                                    | Concise data                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  | Earlier FC06 dependencies                                                                            |

| `FC06-C01-U001` | **Convention/construction — affine space**                                                  | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L330                          | Fix an algebraically closed field k. Affine n-space A^n_k is k^n; its elements are points with affine coordinates.                                                                                                                            | —                                                                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U002` | **Construction — polynomial zero sets**                                                     | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L332–338                      | For A=k[x_1,…,x_n], polynomials are functions on A^n; for T⊆A, Z(T) is their common zero set, depending only on the ideal generated by T.                                                                                                     | `FC06-C01-U001`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U003` | **Definition — affine algebraic set**                                                       | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L340                          | Y⊆A^n is algebraic when Y=Z(T) for some T⊆k[x_1,…,x_n].                                                                                                                                                                                       | `FC06-C01-U002`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U004` | **Proposition 1.1 — algebraic sets are closed-set data**                                    | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L342                          | Algebraic sets are closed under arbitrary intersections and finite unions, and include ∅ and A^n.                                                                                                                                             | `FC06-C01-U003`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U005` | **Definition — Zariski topology on affine space**                                           | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L348                          | The closed sets of A^n are the algebraic sets; equivalently opens are their complements.                                                                                                                                                      | `FC06-C01-U003`, `FC06-C01-U004`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U006` | **Example 1.1.1 — Zariski topology on A^1**                                                 | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L350                          | Over algebraically closed k, proper closed subsets of A^1 are finite, so nonempty opens are complements of finite sets; the topology is not Hausdorff.                                                                                        | `FC06-C01-U005`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U007` | **Definition — irreducible subset**                                                         | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L352                          | A nonempty subspace Y is irreducible when it is not the union of two proper closed subsets of Y.                                                                                                                                              | —                                                                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U008` | **Example 1.1.2 — A^1 is irreducible**                                                      | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L354                          | A^1 is irreducible because every proper closed subset is finite whereas A^1 is infinite.                                                                                                                                                      | `FC06-C01-U006`, `FC06-C01-U007`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U009` | **Example 1.1.3 — open subsets of irreducible spaces**                                      | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L356                          | Every nonempty open subset of an irreducible space is irreducible and dense.                                                                                                                                                                  | `FC06-C01-U007`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U010` | **Example 1.1.4 — closure preserves irreducibility**                                        | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L358                          | The closure of an irreducible subset is irreducible.                                                                                                                                                                                          | `FC06-C01-U007`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U011` | **Definition — affine and quasi-affine varieties**                                          | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L360                          | An affine variety is an irreducible closed subset of A^n; a quasi-affine variety is an open subset of an affine variety.                                                                                                                      | `FC06-C01-U003`, `FC06-C01-U007`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U012` | **Construction — ideal of a subset**                                                        | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L364–368                      | For Y⊆A^n, I(Y) is the ideal of polynomials vanishing at every point of Y.                                                                                                                                                                    | `FC06-C01-U002`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U013` | **Proposition 1.2 — zero-set/vanishing-ideal calculus**                                     | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L370–383                      | Z and I reverse inclusion; I(Y_1∪Y_2)=I(Y_1)∩I(Y_2), I(Z(a))=√a, and Z(I(Y)) is the closure of Y.                                                                                                                                             | `FC06-C01-U002`, `FC06-C01-U012`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U014` | **Theorem 1.3A — Hilbert Nullstellensatz**                                                  | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L385                          | If k is algebraically closed, a⊆k[x_1,…,x_n], and f vanishes on Z(a), then f^r∈a for some r>0.                                                                                                                                                | `FC06-C01-U002`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U015` | **Corollary 1.4 — algebraic sets versus radical ideals**                                    | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L389                          | Y↦I(Y) and a↦Z(a) give an inclusion-reversing bijection between algebraic sets and radical ideals; irreducible algebraic sets correspond to prime ideals.                                                                                     | `FC06-C01-U007`, `FC06-C01-U013`, `FC06-C01-U014`                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U016` | **Example 1.4.1 — affine space is irreducible**                                             | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L395                          | A^n is irreducible because its ideal is (0), which is prime.                                                                                                                                                                                  | `FC06-C01-U015`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U017` | **Example 1.4.2 — irreducible plane curves**                                                | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L397                          | An irreducible f∈k[x,y] defines an irreducible affine curve Z(f); its degree is deg f.                                                                                                                                                        | `FC06-C01-U015`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U018` | **Example 1.4.3 — affine hypersurfaces**                                                    | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L399                          | An irreducible polynomial in k[x_1,…,x_n] defines an affine hypersurface; for n=3 Hartshorne calls it a surface.                                                                                                                              | `FC06-C01-U017`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U019` | **Example 1.4.4 — maximal ideals are points**                                               | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L401                          | Every maximal ideal of k[x_1,…,x_n] has the form (x_1-a_1,…,x_n-a_n) for a unique point (a_1,…,a_n)∈A^n.                                                                                                                                      | `FC06-C01-U015`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U020` | **Example 1.4.5 — algebraic closure is essential**                                          | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L403                          | Over a non-algebraically-closed field, the affine Nullstellensatz correspondence can fail; Hartshorne gives a real plane curve with no real points.                                                                                           | `FC06-C01-U013`, `FC06-C01-U014`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U021` | **Definition — affine coordinate ring**                                                     | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L405                          | For affine algebraic Y⊆A^n, its affine coordinate ring is A(Y)=k[x_1,…,x_n]/I(Y).                                                                                                                                                             | `FC06-C01-U012`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U022` | **Remark 1.4.6 — affine varieties and finitely generated domains**                          | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L407                          | A(Y) is a finitely generated k-domain for an affine variety Y; conversely every finitely generated k-domain is an affine coordinate ring.                                                                                                     | `FC06-C01-U011`, `FC06-C01-U015`, `FC06-C01-U021`                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U023` | **Definition — noetherian topological space**                                               | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L411                          | X is noetherian when every descending chain of closed subsets stabilizes.                                                                                                                                                                     | —                                                                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U024` | **Example 1.4.7 — affine space is noetherian**                                              | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L413                          | A^n is noetherian because descending chains of closed sets give ascending chains of vanishing ideals in the noetherian polynomial ring.                                                                                                       | `FC06-C01-U013`, `FC06-C01-U023`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U025` | **Proposition 1.5 — irreducible components in noetherian spaces**                           | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L415                          | Every nonempty closed subset of a noetherian space is a finite union of irreducible closed subsets; after removing inclusions, the components are unique.                                                                                     | `FC06-C01-U007`, `FC06-C01-U023`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U026` | **Corollary 1.6 — decomposition of affine algebraic sets**                                  | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L421                          | Every affine algebraic set decomposes uniquely as a finite union of varieties, none containing another.                                                                                                                                       | `FC06-C01-U011`, `FC06-C01-U024`, `FC06-C01-U025`                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U027` | **Definition — dimension of a topological space/variety**                                   | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L423                          | dim X is the supremum of lengths n of strict chains Z_0⊊⋯⊊Z_n of irreducible closed subsets; varieties inherit this dimension.                                                                                                                | `FC06-C01-U007`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U028` | **Example 1.6.1 — dim A^1=1**                                                               | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L425                          | The only irreducible closed subsets of A^1 are points and A^1, hence dim A^1=1.                                                                                                                                                               | `FC06-C01-U006`, `FC06-C01-U027`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U029` | **Definition — height and Krull dimension**                                                 | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L427                          | The height of a prime is the supremum of lengths of prime chains ending there; dim A is the supremum of prime heights.                                                                                                                        | —                                                                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U030` | **Proposition 1.7 — geometric and ring dimension agree**                                    | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L429                          | For an affine algebraic set Y, dim Y=dim A(Y).                                                                                                                                                                                                | `FC06-C01-U015`, `FC06-C01-U021`, `FC06-C01-U027`, `FC06-C01-U029`                                   |
| `FC06-C01-U031` | **Theorem 1.8A — dimension theorem for finitely generated domains**                         | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L435                          | If B is a finitely generated k-domain, dim B=trdeg_k Frac(B), and for every prime p, ht p+dim(B/p)=dim B.                                                                                                                                     | `FC06-C01-U029`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U032` | **Proposition 1.9 — dimension of affine space**                                             | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L443                          | dim A^n=n.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    | `FC06-C01-U030`, `FC06-C01-U031`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U033` | **Proposition 1.10 — quasi-affine dimension equals closure dimension**                      | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L447                          | If Y is quasi-affine, then dim Y=dim \bar Y.                                                                                                                                                                                                  | `FC06-C01-U009`, `FC06-C01-U027`, `FC06-C01-U030`, `FC06-C01-U031`                                   |
| `FC06-C01-U034` | **Theorem 1.11A — Krull Hauptidealsatz**                                                    | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L451                          | In a noetherian ring, every minimal prime over a nonzerodivisor nonunit f has height 1.                                                                                                                                                       | —                                                                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U035` | **Proposition 1.12A — height-one criterion for UFDs**                                       | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L455                          | A noetherian domain is a UFD iff every height-one prime ideal is principal.                                                                                                                                                                   | `FC06-C01-U029`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U036` | **Proposition 1.13 — codimension-one affine varieties are hypersurfaces**                   | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L459                          | A variety Y⊆A^n has dimension n−1 iff Y=Z(f) for a nonconstant irreducible polynomial f.                                                                                                                                                      | `FC06-C01-U030`, `FC06-C01-U031`, `FC06-C01-U034`, `FC06-C01-U035`                                   |
| `FC06-C01-U037` | **Remark 1.13.1 — height two need not be two-generated**                                    | §1 Affine Varieties; pp. 1–7; L463                          | A height-two prime ideal in a polynomial ring need not be generated by two elements.                                                                                                                                                          | `FC06-C01-U035`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U038` | **Definition/construction — projective space and homogeneous coordinates**                  | §2 Projective Varieties; pp. 8–13; L495–497                 | P^n_k is (A^{n+1}{0}) modulo nonzero scalar multiplication; representatives are homogeneous coordinates.                                                                                                                                      | `FC06-C01-U001`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U039` | **Definitions — graded ring, homogeneous elements and homogeneous ideals**                  | §2 Projective Varieties; pp. 8–13; L501                     | A graded ring S=⊕*{d≥0}S_d has S_dS_e⊆S*{d+e}; homogeneous elements lie in one degree and homogeneous ideals are graded submodules (equivalently homogeneously generated).                                                                    | —                                                                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U040` | **Construction — projective zero sets**                                                     | §2 Projective Varieties; pp. 8–13; L503–511                 | Homogeneous polynomials have well-defined vanishing on P^n; for homogeneous T, Z(T) is the common projective zero set, and Z(a) uses the homogeneous elements of a homogeneous ideal.                                                         | `FC06-C01-U038`, `FC06-C01-U039`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U041` | **Definition — projective algebraic set**                                                   | §2 Projective Varieties; pp. 8–13; L513                     | Y⊆P^n is algebraic when Y=Z(T) for a set T of homogeneous polynomials.                                                                                                                                                                        | `FC06-C01-U040`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U042` | **Proposition 2.1 — projective algebraic sets are closed-set data**                         | §2 Projective Varieties; pp. 8–13; L515                     | Projective algebraic sets are closed under arbitrary intersections and finite unions, and include ∅ and P^n.                                                                                                                                  | `FC06-C01-U041`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U043` | **Definition — Zariski topology on projective space**                                       | §2 Projective Varieties; pp. 8–13; L519                     | The projective Zariski topology takes algebraic sets as closed sets.                                                                                                                                                                          | `FC06-C01-U041`, `FC06-C01-U042`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U044` | **Definition — projective and quasi-projective varieties**                                  | §2 Projective Varieties; pp. 8–13; L523                     | A projective variety is an irreducible projective algebraic set; a quasi-projective variety is an open subset of one; dimension is topological dimension.                                                                                     | `FC06-C01-U007`, `FC06-C01-U027`, `FC06-C01-U041`, `FC06-C01-U043`                                   |
| `FC06-C01-U045` | **Construction — homogeneous ideal and coordinate ring**                                    | §2 Projective Varieties; pp. 8–13; L525                     | For Y⊆P^n, I(Y) is generated by homogeneous polynomials vanishing on Y; for algebraic Y, S(Y)=k[x_0,…,x_n]/I(Y).                                                                                                                              | `FC06-C01-U039`, `FC06-C01-U040`, `FC06-C01-U041`                                                    |

| `FC06-C01-U049` | **Definition — regular functions on quasi-affine varieties**                                | §3 Morphisms; pp. 14–23; L624                               | f:Y→k is regular at P if locally f=g/h with g,h polynomial and h nowhere zero; it is regular when so at every point.                                                                                                                          | `FC06-C01-U011`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U050` | **Lemma 3.1 — regular functions are continuous**                                            | §3 Morphisms; pp. 14–23; L626                               | A regular function on a quasi-affine variety is continuous for the Zariski topology on k=A^1.                                                                                                                                                 | `FC06-C01-U005`, `FC06-C01-U049`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U051` | **Definition — regular functions on quasi-projective varieties**                            | §3 Morphisms; pp. 14–23; L632                               | f:Y→k is regular at P if locally f=g/h for homogeneous g,h of the same degree with h nowhere zero.                                                                                                                                            | `FC06-C01-U044`, `FC06-C01-U040`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U052` | **Remark 3.1.1 — identity principle for regular functions**                                 | §3 Morphisms; pp. 14–23; L634                               | Regular functions on a variety that agree on a nonempty open subset agree everywhere.                                                                                                                                                         | `FC06-C01-U007`, `FC06-C01-U009`, `FC06-C01-U050`, `FC06-C01-U051`                                   |
| `FC06-C01-U053` | **Definition — varieties and morphisms**                                                    | §3 Morphisms; pp. 14–23; L638                               | A variety means affine, quasi-affine, projective, or quasi-projective; a morphism is continuous and pulls regular functions on target opens back to regular functions.                                                                        | `FC06-C01-U011`, `FC06-C01-U044`, `FC06-C01-U049`, `FC06-C01-U051`                                   |
| `FC06-C01-U054` | **Definition/construction — isomorphism of varieties**                                      | §3 Morphisms; pp. 14–23; L640                               | Morphisms compose; an isomorphism is a morphism admitting a two-sided inverse morphism.                                                                                                                                                       | `FC06-C01-U053`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U055` | **Definitions — global and local rings of regular functions**                               | §3 Morphisms; pp. 14–23; L644                               | O(Y) is the ring of global regular functions; O_{P,Y} is the local ring of germs of regular functions near P.                                                                                                                                 | `FC06-C01-U049`, `FC06-C01-U051`, `FC06-C01-U053`                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U056` | **Construction — maximal ideal and residue field of O_{P,Y}**                               | §3 Morphisms; pp. 14–23; L646                               | O_{P,Y} is local: its maximal ideal consists of germs vanishing at P, and its residue field is k.                                                                                                                                             | `FC06-C01-U055`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U057` | **Definition — function field and rational functions**                                      | §3 Morphisms; pp. 14–23; L648                               | K(Y) is the equivalence classes of regular functions on nonempty opens, identified when equal on overlap; its elements are rational functions.                                                                                                | `FC06-C01-U055`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U058` | **Proposition/construction — K(Y) is a field**                                              | §3 Morphisms; pp. 14–23; L650                               | Irreducibility makes nonempty opens intersect, so rational functions add and multiply; a nonzero rational function is invertible after restricting away from its zero set.                                                                    | `FC06-C01-U007`, `FC06-C01-U057`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U059` | **Construction — O(Y) and local rings inside K(Y)**                                         | §3 Morphisms; pp. 14–23; L652                               | Restriction gives injective maps O(Y)→O_{P,Y}→K(Y), so these rings are viewed as subrings of the function field.                                                                                                                              | `FC06-C01-U052`, `FC06-C01-U055`, `FC06-C01-U057`                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U060` | **Comparison — function rings are isomorphism invariants**                                  | §3 Morphisms; pp. 14–23; L654                               | Replacing Y by an isomorphic variety preserves O(Y), O_{P,Y}, and K(Y) up to isomorphism.                                                                                                                                                     | `FC06-C01-U054`, `FC06-C01-U055`, `FC06-C01-U057`                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U061` | **Theorem 3.2 — affine function-ring dictionary**                                           | §3 Morphisms; pp. 14–23; L660                               | For affine Y: O(Y)≅A(Y); points correspond to maximal ideals m_P; O_{P,Y}≅A(Y)*{m_P} with dim O*{P,Y}=dim Y; and K(Y)=Frac A(Y), with trdeg_k K(Y)=dim Y.                                                                                     | `FC06-C01-U015`, `FC06-C01-U021`, `FC06-C01-U030`, `FC06-C01-U031`, `FC06-C01-U055`, `FC06-C01-U057` |
| `FC06-C01-U062` | **Proposition 3.3 — standard projective charts are affine varieties**                       | §3 Morphisms; pp. 14–23; L680                               | The homeomorphism φ_i:U_i→A^n of Proposition 2.2 is an isomorphism of varieties.                                                                                                                                                              | `FC06-C01-U047`, `FC06-C01-U053`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U063` | **Convention/notation — degree-zero homogeneous localizations**                             | §3 Morphisms; pp. 14–23; L684                               | For homogeneous prime p in graded S, S_(p) is the degree-zero subring of the homogeneous localization; similarly S_(f) for homogeneous f.                                                                                                     | `FC06-C01-U039`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U064` | **Theorem 3.4 — projective function-ring dictionary**                                       | §3 Morphisms; pp. 14–23; L686                               | For projective Y with homogeneous ring S(Y): O(Y)=k, O_{P,Y}≅S(Y)*(m_P), and K(Y)≅S(Y)*((0)).                                                                                                                                                 | `FC06-C01-U045`, `FC06-C01-U055`, `FC06-C01-U057`, `FC06-C01-U061`, `FC06-C01-U062`, `FC06-C01-U063` |

| `FC06-C01-U072` | **Definition — birational map and birational equivalence**                                  | §4 Rational Maps; pp. 24–30; L849                           | A birational map is a rational map admitting a two-sided inverse rational map; varieties joined by one are birationally equivalent.                                                                                                           | `FC06-C01-U071`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U073` | **Lemma 4.2 — principal affine opens**                                                      | §4 Rational Maps; pp. 24–30; L853                           | If Y=Z(f)⊆A^n is a hypersurface, then A^n\Y is affine, isomorphic to zf=1 in A^{n+1}, with coordinate ring k[x_1,…,x_n]_f.                                                                                                                    | `FC06-C01-U021`, `FC06-C01-U066`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U074` | **Proposition 4.3 — affine opens form a basis**                                             | §4 Rational Maps; pp. 24–30; L857                           | Every variety has a basis of open subsets that are affine varieties.                                                                                                                                                                          | `FC06-C01-U048`, `FC06-C01-U073`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U075` | **Construction — pullback on function fields from a dominant rational map**                 | §4 Rational Maps; pp. 24–30; L861                           | A dominant rational map φ:X⇢Y induces an injective k-algebra map φ*:K(Y)→K(X) by composition on suitable nonempty opens.                                                                                                                      | `FC06-C01-U057`, `FC06-C01-U071`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U076` | **Theorem 4.4 — varieties and function fields**                                             | §4 Rational Maps; pp. 24–30; L863–867                       | Dominant rational maps X⇢Y correspond bijectively to k-algebra maps K(Y)→K(X), yielding an arrow-reversing equivalence with finitely generated field extensions of k.                                                                         | `FC06-C01-U061`, `FC06-C01-U074`, `FC06-C01-U075`                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U077` | **Corollary 4.5 — characterizations of birational equivalence**                             | §4 Rational Maps; pp. 24–30; L873–876                       | X and Y are birational iff they contain isomorphic nonempty opens iff K(X)≅K(Y) as k-algebras.                                                                                                                                                | `FC06-C01-U054`, `FC06-C01-U072`, `FC06-C01-U076`                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U078` | **Theorem 4.6A — primitive element theorem**                                                | §4 Rational Maps; pp. 24–30; L887                           | A finite separable extension L/K is simple; if K is infinite, a primitive element can be chosen as a K-linear combination of any finite generating set.                                                                                       | —                                                                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U079` | **Definition — separably generated field extension**                                        | §4 Rational Maps; pp. 24–30; L891                           | K/k is separably generated when K is separable algebraic over k(B) for some transcendence basis B; such B is a separating transcendence basis.                                                                                                | —                                                                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U080` | **Theorem 4.7A — separating bases inside generators**                                       | §4 Rational Maps; pp. 24–30; L893                           | If K/k is finitely generated and separably generated, every generating set contains a separating transcendence basis.                                                                                                                         | `FC06-C01-U079`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U081` | **Theorem 4.8A — finite-type extensions of perfect fields are separably generated**         | §4 Rational Maps; pp. 24–30; L897                           | If k is perfect, in particular algebraically closed, every finitely generated field extension K/k is separably generated.                                                                                                                     | `FC06-C01-U079`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U082` | **Proposition 4.9 — hypersurface birational model**                                         | §4 Rational Maps; pp. 24–30; L901                           | Every r-dimensional variety is birational to a hypersurface in P^{r+1}.                                                                                                                                                                       | `FC06-C01-U031`, `FC06-C01-U077`, `FC06-C01-U078`, `FC06-C01-U080`, `FC06-C01-U081`                  |
| `FC06-C01-U083` | **Construction — blowup of A^n at the origin**                                              | §4 Rational Maps; pp. 24–30; L911–917                       | The blowup X⊆A^n×P^{n−1} is cut out by x_i y_j=x_j y_i; projection to A^n gives the blowup morphism φ.                                                                                                                                        | `FC06-C01-U038`, `FC06-C01-U053`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U084` | **Property — blowup is an isomorphism off the center**                                      | §4 Rational Maps; pp. 24–30; L919                           | φ restricts to X\φ^{-1}(O)≅A^n{O}.                                                                                                                                                                                                            | `FC06-C01-U083`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U085` | **Property — exceptional fibre of the affine-space blowup**                                 | §4 Rational Maps; pp. 24–30; L921                           | The fibre φ^{-1}(O) is P^{n−1}.                                                                                                                                                                                                               | `FC06-C01-U083`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U086` | **Property — exceptional directions**                                                       | §4 Rational Maps; pp. 24–30; L923                           | Points of φ^{-1}(O) correspond bijectively to lines through O in A^n via their limiting direction.                                                                                                                                            | `FC06-C01-U083`, `FC06-C01-U085`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U087` | **Property — irreducibility of the affine-space blowup**                                    | §4 Rational Maps; pp. 24–30; L925                           | The blowup X of A^n at O is irreducible; the complement of the exceptional fibre is dense.                                                                                                                                                    | `FC06-C01-U007`, `FC06-C01-U084`, `FC06-C01-U086`                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U088` | **Definition — blowup of a subvariety at a point**                                          | §4 Rational Maps; pp. 24–30; L930                           | For closed Y⊆A^n through O, the blowup \tilde Y is the closure of φ^{-1}(Y{O}) in the blowup of A^n; φ restricts to \tilde Y→Y.                                                                                                               | `FC06-C01-U083`, `FC06-C01-U084`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U089` | **Comparison — blowup morphism is birational**                                              | §4 Rational Maps; pp. 24–30; L932                           | \tilde Y\φ^{-1}(O)≅Y{O}, hence φ:\tilde Y→Y is birational; Hartshorne notes the construction will later be shown intrinsic.                                                                                                                   | `FC06-C01-U072`, `FC06-C01-U088`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U090` | **Example 4.9.1 — blowup of a nodal plane cubic**                                           | §4 Rational Maps; pp. 24–30; L936–944                       | Blowing up the nodal cubic y²=x²(x+1) replaces O by an exceptional P^1; the strict transform meets it in the two points corresponding to the two branch slopes.                                                                               | `FC06-C01-U083`, `FC06-C01-U085`, `FC06-C01-U088`                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U091` | **Construction/comparison — strict transform under a blowup**                               | §4 Rational Maps; pp. 24–30; L946–948                       | The strict transform of a curve through the center is the closure of its inverse image off the center; distinct tangent directions meet the exceptional curve at distinct points.                                                             | `FC06-C01-U084`, `FC06-C01-U085`, `FC06-C01-U088`, `FC06-C01-U090`                                   |
| `FC06-C01-U092` | **Definition — Jacobian nonsingularity for affine varieties**                               | §5 Nonsingular Varieties; pp. 31–38; L986                   | If Y⊆A^n has dimension r and ideal generators f_i, Y is nonsingular at P when the Jacobian (∂f_i/∂x_j)(P) has rank n−r.                                                                                                                       | `FC06-C01-U011`, `FC06-C01-U027`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U093` | **Definition/notation — Jacobian matrix at a point**                                        | §5 Nonsingular Varieties; pp. 31–38; L990                   | The matrix (∂f_i/∂x_j)(P) is the Jacobian matrix; the nonsingularity condition is independent of the chosen ideal generators.                                                                                                                 | `FC06-C01-U092`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U094` | **Definition — regular local ring**                                                         | §5 Nonsingular Varieties; pp. 31–38; L994                   | A noetherian local ring (A,m,k) is regular when dim_k(m/m²)=dim A.                                                                                                                                                                            | —                                                                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U095` | **Theorem 5.1 — intrinsic criterion for nonsingularity**                                    | §5 Nonsingular Varieties; pp. 31–38; L996                   | For an affine variety Y and P∈Y, Y is nonsingular at P iff O_{P,Y} is a regular local ring.                                                                                                                                                   | `FC06-C01-U055`, `FC06-C01-U061`, `FC06-C01-U092`, `FC06-C01-U094`                                   |
| `FC06-C01-U096` | **Definition — nonsingular and singular arbitrary varieties**                               | §5 Nonsingular Varieties; pp. 31–38; L1020                  | A variety is nonsingular at P when O_P is regular; it is nonsingular if so everywhere and singular otherwise.                                                                                                                                 | `FC06-C01-U055`, `FC06-C01-U094`, `FC06-C01-U095`                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U097` | **Proposition 5.2A — embedding-dimension inequality**                                       | §5 Nonsingular Varieties; pp. 31–38; L1024                  | For a noetherian local ring (A,m,k), dim_k(m/m²)≥dim A.                                                                                                                                                                                       | `FC06-C01-U094`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U098` | **Theorem 5.3 — singular locus is proper closed**                                           | §5 Nonsingular Varieties; pp. 31–38; L1028                  | For every variety Y, Sing Y is a proper closed subset of Y.                                                                                                                                                                                   | `FC06-C01-U074`, `FC06-C01-U082`, `FC06-C01-U092`, `FC06-C01-U096`, `FC06-C01-U097`                  |
| `FC06-C01-U099` | **Construction — m-adic completion**                                                        | §5 Nonsingular Varieties; pp. 31–38; L1040                  | For a local ring (A,m), the m-adic completion \hat A is the completion for powers of m, equivalently lim← A/m^n.                                                                                                                              | —                                                                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U100` | **Theorem 5.4A — basic properties of completion**                                           | §5 Nonsingular Varieties; pp. 31–38; L1046                  | For noetherian local A: \hat A is local and A→\hat A injective; \hat M≅M⊗_A\hat A for finite M; dim \hat A=dim A; regularity is preserved and reflected.                                                                                      | `FC06-C01-U094`, `FC06-C01-U099`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U101` | **Theorem 5.5A — Cohen structure theorem, regular case**                                    | §5 Nonsingular Varieties; pp. 31–38; L1050                  | A complete regular local ring of dimension n containing a field is isomorphic to kx_1,…,x_n for its residue field k.                                                                                                                      | `FC06-C01-U094`, `FC06-C01-U100`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U102` | **Definition — analytically isomorphic points**                                             | §5 Nonsingular Varieties; pp. 31–38; L1055                  | P∈X and Q∈Y are analytically isomorphic when their completed local rings are isomorphic as k-algebras.                                                                                                                                        | `FC06-C01-U055`, `FC06-C01-U099`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U103` | **Example 5.6.1 — analytic isomorphism preserves dimension**                                | §5 Nonsingular Varieties; pp. 31–38; L1057                  | Analytically isomorphic points lie on varieties of the same dimension.                                                                                                                                                                        | `FC06-C01-U061`, `FC06-C01-U100`, `FC06-C01-U102`                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U104` | **Example 5.6.2 — smooth points have one analytic type by dimension**                       | §5 Nonsingular Varieties; pp. 31–38; L1059                  | Nonsingular points on varieties of the same dimension are analytically isomorphic.                                                                                                                                                            | `FC06-C01-U096`, `FC06-C01-U100`, `FC06-C01-U101`, `FC06-C01-U102`                                   |
| `FC06-C01-U105` | **Example 5.6.3 — analytic type of a node**                                                 | §5 Nonsingular Varieties; pp. 31–38; L1061–1083             | In characteristic ≠2, the node of y²=x²(x+1) has completed local ring isomorphic to kx,y/(xy), so analytically it is two transverse branches.                                                                                             | `FC06-C01-U102`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U106` | **Comparison — completion can destroy integrality**                                         | §5 Nonsingular Varieties; pp. 31–38; L1085                  | In Example 5.6.3 the local ring is a domain whereas its completion is not.                                                                                                                                                                    | `FC06-C01-U105`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U107` | **Theorem 5.7A — elimination theory**                                                       | §5 Nonsingular Varieties; pp. 31–38; L1089                  | For homogeneous forms with indeterminate coefficients there are universal integer polynomials in those coefficients whose simultaneous vanishing is equivalent, after specialization to any field, to the forms having a common nonzero zero. | —                                                                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U108` | **Convention — function field of dimension one**                                            | §6 Nonsingular Curves; pp. 39–46; L1181                     | A finitely generated extension K/k of transcendence degree one is called a function field of dimension one.                                                                                                                                   | `FC06-C01-U057`, `FC06-C01-U061`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U109` | **Definition — valuation**                                                                  | §6 Nonsingular Curves; pp. 39–46; L1187–1191                | A valuation v:K^×→G to a totally ordered abelian group satisfies v(xy)=v(x)+v(y) and v(x+y)≥min(v(x),v(y)).                                                                                                                                   | —                                                                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U110` | **Definitions — valuation ring and valuation over k**                                       | §6 Nonsingular Curves; pp. 39–46; L1193                     | R={x:v(x)≥0}∪{0} is the valuation ring with maximal ideal {x:v(x)>0}∪{0}; R is a valuation ring of K if K is its fraction field, and of K/k when v is trivial on k^×.                                                                         | `FC06-C01-U109`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U111` | **Definition — domination of local rings**                                                  | §6 Nonsingular Curves; pp. 39–46; L1195                     | For local subrings A,B⊆K, B dominates A when A⊆B and m_B∩A=m_A.                                                                                                                                                                               | `FC06-C01-U110`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U112` | **Theorem 6.1A — valuation rings via domination**                                           | §6 Nonsingular Curves; pp. 39–46; L1197                     | A local subring R⊆K is a valuation ring iff it is maximal under domination; every local subring of K is dominated by a valuation ring.                                                                                                        | `FC06-C01-U110`, `FC06-C01-U111`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U113` | **Definition — discrete valuation and DVR**                                                 | §6 Nonsingular Curves; pp. 39–46; L1201                     | A valuation is discrete when its value group is Z; its valuation ring is a discrete valuation ring.                                                                                                                                           | `FC06-C01-U109`, `FC06-C01-U110`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U114` | **Theorem 6.2A — one-dimensional local-domain characterizations of DVRs**                   | §6 Nonsingular Curves; pp. 39–46; L1203–1205                | For a one-dimensional noetherian local domain A, the following are equivalent: DVR; integrally closed; regular local; maximal ideal principal.                                                                                                | `FC06-C01-U094`, `FC06-C01-U113`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U115` | **Definition — Dedekind domain**                                                            | §6 Nonsingular Curves; pp. 39–46; L1209                     | A Dedekind domain is an integrally closed noetherian domain of dimension one.                                                                                                                                                                 | `FC06-C01-U029`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U116` | **Comparison — localizations of Dedekind domains are DVRs**                                 | §6 Nonsingular Curves; pp. 39–46; L1211                     | Localizing a Dedekind domain at any nonzero prime ideal gives a discrete valuation ring.                                                                                                                                                      | `FC06-C01-U113`, `FC06-C01-U114`, `FC06-C01-U115`                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U117` | **Theorem 6.3A — integral closure preserves Dedekind domains in finite extensions**         | §6 Nonsingular Curves; pp. 39–46; L1213                     | The integral closure of a Dedekind domain in a finite extension of its fraction field is Dedekind.                                                                                                                                            | `FC06-C01-U115`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U118` | **Comparison — local rings on nonsingular curves are DVRs**                                 | §6 Nonsingular Curves; pp. 39–46; L1217                     | If P lies on a nonsingular curve Y, then O_{P,Y} is a DVR of K(Y)/k.                                                                                                                                                                          | `FC06-C01-U061`, `FC06-C01-U095`, `FC06-C01-U113`, `FC06-C01-U114`                                   |
| `FC06-C01-U119` | **Lemma 6.4 — local-ring containment separates curve points**                               | §6 Nonsingular Curves; pp. 39–46; L1219                     | For a quasi-projective variety Y, O_Q⊆O_P inside K(Y) implies P=Q.                                                                                                                                                                            | `FC06-C01-U055`, `FC06-C01-U057`, `FC06-C01-U061`                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U120` | **Lemma 6.5 — finiteness of poles across DVRs**                                             | §6 Nonsingular Curves; pp. 39–46; L1225                     | For a dimension-one function field K/k and x∈K, only finitely many DVRs R of K/k fail to contain x.                                                                                                                                           | `FC06-C01-U069`, `FC06-C01-U108`, `FC06-C01-U112`, `FC06-C01-U113`, `FC06-C01-U117`                  |
| `FC06-C01-U121` | **Corollary 6.6 — DVRs occur as local rings on nonsingular affine curves**                  | §6 Nonsingular Curves; pp. 39–46; L1235                     | Every DVR of a dimension-one function field K/k is isomorphic to O_P for a point P on some nonsingular affine curve with function field K.                                                                                                    | `FC06-C01-U061`, `FC06-C01-U096`, `FC06-C01-U120`                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U122` | **Construction — the valuation curve C_K**                                                  | §6 Nonsingular Curves; pp. 39–46; L1239                     | C_K is the set of DVRs R_P of K/k, with finite subsets and C_K closed; for open U, O(U)=∩_{P∈U}R_P. Evaluation uses residues, and its function field is K.                                                                                    | `FC06-C01-U108`, `FC06-C01-U113`, `FC06-C01-U119`, `FC06-C01-U120`, `FC06-C01-U121`                  |
| `FC06-C01-U123` | **Definition — abstract nonsingular curve**                                                 | §6 Nonsingular Curves; pp. 39–46; L1241                     | An abstract nonsingular curve is an open subset U⊆C_K with the induced topology and regular functions.                                                                                                                                        | `FC06-C01-U122`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U124` | **Definition — morphisms involving abstract nonsingular curves**                            | §6 Nonsingular Curves; pp. 39–46; L1245                     | A morphism between abstract nonsingular curves or varieties is continuous and pulls regular functions back to regular functions.                                                                                                              | `FC06-C01-U053`, `FC06-C01-U123`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U125` | **Proposition 6.7 — quasi-projective nonsingular curves are abstract curves**               | §6 Nonsingular Curves; pp. 39–46; L1249                     | Every nonsingular quasi-projective curve is isomorphic to an open subset of C_{K(Y)}.                                                                                                                                                         | `FC06-C01-U118`, `FC06-C01-U119`, `FC06-C01-U122`, `FC06-C01-U123`, `FC06-C01-U124`                  |
| `FC06-C01-U126` | **Proposition 6.8 — extension across a missing point**                                      | §6 Nonsingular Curves; pp. 39–46; L1259                     | If X is an abstract nonsingular curve, P∈X, Y is projective, and φ:X{P}→Y is a morphism, φ extends uniquely to X→Y.                                                                                                                           | `FC06-C01-U070`, `FC06-C01-U123`, `FC06-C01-U124`                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U127` | **Theorem 6.9 — projective realization of C_K**                                             | §6 Nonsingular Curves; pp. 39–46; L1283                     | For every dimension-one function field K/k, the abstract curve C_K is isomorphic to a nonsingular projective curve.                                                                                                                           | `FC06-C01-U121`, `FC06-C01-U125`, `FC06-C01-U126`                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U128` | **Corollary 6.10 — abstract and quasi-projective nonsingular curves coincide**              | §6 Nonsingular Curves; pp. 39–46; L1307                     | Every abstract nonsingular curve is quasi-projective, and every nonsingular quasi-projective curve is an open subset of a nonsingular projective curve.                                                                                       | `FC06-C01-U125`, `FC06-C01-U127`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U129` | **Corollary 6.11 — nonsingular projective model of every curve**                            | §6 Nonsingular Curves; pp. 39–46; L1309                     | Every curve is birationally equivalent to a nonsingular projective curve.                                                                                                                                                                     | `FC06-C01-U077`, `FC06-C01-U127`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U130` | **Corollary 6.12 — three equivalent curve categories**                                      | §6 Nonsingular Curves; pp. 39–46; L1313–1317                | Nonsingular projective curves with dominant morphisms, quasi-projective curves with dominant rational maps, and dimension-one function fields with k-homomorphisms are equivalent categories.                                                 | `FC06-C01-U076`, `FC06-C01-U126`, `FC06-C01-U127`, `FC06-C01-U129`                                   |
| `FC06-C01-U131` | **Proposition 7.1 — affine dimension theorem**                                              | §7 Intersections in Projective Space; pp. 47–54; L1373      | If Y,Z⊆A^n have dimensions r,s, every irreducible component of Y∩Z has dimension at least r+s−n.                                                                                                                                              | `FC06-C01-U030`, `FC06-C01-U031`, `FC06-C01-U034`                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U132` | **Theorem 7.2 — projective dimension theorem**                                              | §7 Intersections in Projective Space; pp. 47–54; L1379      | If Y,Z⊆P^n have dimensions r,s, every component of Y∩Z has dimension ≥r+s−n; if r+s−n≥0 then Y∩Z is nonempty.                                                                                                                                 | `FC06-C01-U044`, `FC06-C01-U131`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U133` | **Definition — numerical polynomial**                                                       | §7 Intersections in Projective Space; pp. 47–54; L1385      | P(z)∈Q[z] is numerical if P(n)∈Z for all sufficiently large integers n.                                                                                                                                                                       | —                                                                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U134` | **Proposition 7.3 — binomial basis and discrete integration for numerical polynomials**     | §7 Intersections in Projective Space; pp. 47–54; L1387–1429 | A numerical polynomial has an integer-coefficient expansion in binomial polynomials; if Δf eventually equals a numerical polynomial, then f eventually agrees with a numerical polynomial.                                                    | `FC06-C01-U133`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U135` | **Definitions — graded module, twist, and annihilator**                                     | §7 Intersections in Projective Space; pp. 47–54; L1431      | A graded S-module M has M=⊕*{d∈Z}M_d with S_dM_e⊆M*{d+e}; M(l)*d=M*{d+l}; Ann M is homogeneous.                                                                                                                                               | `FC06-C01-U039`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U136` | **Proposition 7.4 — prime filtration of a finite graded module**                            | §7 Intersections in Projective Space; pp. 47–54; L1435–1439 | A finite graded module over noetherian graded S has a filtration with quotients (S/p_i)(l_i); the minimal p_i are the minimal primes of M, and their multiplicities equal lengths of M_p.                                                     | `FC06-C01-U135`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U137` | **Definition — multiplicity at a minimal prime**                                            | §7 Intersections in Projective Space; pp. 47–54; L1447      | For a minimal prime p of graded M, μ_p(M)=length_{S_p}(M_p).                                                                                                                                                                                  | `FC06-C01-U136`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U138` | **Definition — Hilbert function**                                                           | §7 Intersections in Projective Space; pp. 47–54; L1449–1455 | For a graded module M over S=k[x_0,…,x_n], the Hilbert function is φ_M(l)=dim_k M_l.                                                                                                                                                          | `FC06-C01-U135`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U139` | **Theorem 7.5 — Hilbert–Serre**                                                             | §7 Intersections in Projective Space; pp. 47–54; L1457      | For finite graded M over k[x_0,…,x_n], φ_M(l)=P_M(l) for l≫0 for a unique P_M∈Q[z], and deg P_M=dim Z(Ann M).                                                                                                                                 | `FC06-C01-U132`, `FC06-C01-U134`, `FC06-C01-U136`, `FC06-C01-U138`                                   |
| `FC06-C01-U140` | **Definition — Hilbert polynomial of a graded module**                                      | §7 Intersections in Projective Space; pp. 47–54; L1463      | The eventual polynomial P_M of Theorem 7.5 is the Hilbert polynomial of M.                                                                                                                                                                    | `FC06-C01-U139`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U141` | **Definitions — Hilbert polynomial and degree of a projective algebraic set**               | §7 Intersections in Projective Space; pp. 47–54; L1465      | For r-dimensional Y⊆P^n, P_Y is the Hilbert polynomial of S(Y); deg Y is r! times the leading coefficient of P_Y.                                                                                                                             | `FC06-C01-U045`, `FC06-C01-U140`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U142` | **Proposition 7.6 — basic properties of degree**                                            | §7 Intersections in Projective Space; pp. 47–54; L1467–1469 | Nonempty projective algebraic sets have positive integral degree; degree is additive for equidimensional unions with smaller-dimensional intersection; deg P^n=1; a degree-d hypersurface has degree d.                                       | `FC06-C01-U134`, `FC06-C01-U141`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U143` | **Definition — intersection multiplicity with a hypersurface**                              | §7 Intersections in Projective Space; pp. 47–54; L1507      | If H does not contain projective Y and Z_j is a component of Y∩H with homogeneous prime p_j, define i(Y,H;Z_j)=μ_{p_j}(S/(I_Y+I_H)).                                                                                                          | `FC06-C01-U132`, `FC06-C01-U137`, `FC06-C01-U141`, `FC06-C01-U142`                                   |
| `FC06-C01-U144` | **Theorem 7.7 — Bézout formula for a hypersurface section**                                 | §7 Intersections in Projective Space; pp. 47–54; L1509–1513 | If H does not contain positive-dimensional projective Y and Z_j are the components of Y∩H, then Σ_j i(Y,H;Z_j)deg Z_j=(deg Y)(deg H).                                                                                                         | `FC06-C01-U139`, `FC06-C01-U142`, `FC06-C01-U143`                                                    |
| `FC06-C01-U145` | **Corollary 7.8 — Bézout theorem for plane curves**                                         | §7 Intersections in Projective Space; pp. 47–54; L1547–1551 | For distinct plane curves Y,Z of degrees d,e meeting at P_j, Σ_j i(Y,Z;P_j)=de.                                                                                                                                                               | `FC06-C01-U144`                                                                                      |
| `FC06-C01-U146` | **Remark 7.8.1 — local and homogeneous intersection multiplicities agree for plane curves** | §7 Intersections in Projective Space; pp. 47–54; L1556      | Hartshorne’s homogeneous-coordinate-ring intersection multiplicity agrees with the earlier local definition for intersections of plane curves.                                                                                                | `FC06-C01-U143`, `FC06-C01-U145`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C01-U147` | **Remark 7.8.2 — Bézout extends to reducible plane curves**                                 | §7 Intersections in Projective Space; pp. 47–54; L1558      | The plane-curve Bézout formula extends to reducible one-dimensional algebraic sets provided they share no irreducible component.                                                                                                              | `FC06-C01-U145`                                                                                      |

## Hartshorne, *Algebraic Geometry* — Chapter II: Schemes

| ID              | Kind / source unit                                                                              | Location                                                  | Concise data                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        | Earlier FC06 dependencies                                                           |
| --------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `FC06-C02-U001` | **Definition — presheaf**                                                                       | §1 Sheaves; pp. 60–68; L1640–1648                         | A presheaf F on X assigns an abelian group F(U) to each open U and compatible restriction maps F(U)→F(V) for V⊆U, with F(∅)=0, identity, and composition axioms.                                                                                                                    | —                                                                                   |
| `FC06-C02-U002` | **Convention/comparison — presheaves as contravariant functors**                                | §1 Sheaves; pp. 60–68; L1650–1652                         | A presheaf is equivalently a contravariant functor Top(X)→Ab; replacing Ab gives presheaves of rings, sets, or objects of another fixed category.                                                                                                                                   | `FC06-C02-U001`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C02-U003` | **Convention/notation — sections and restriction**                                              | §1 Sheaves; pp. 60–68; L1654                              | Elements of F(U) are sections over U, also written Γ(U,F); restriction maps are written s↦s\|_V.                                                                                                                                                                                    | `FC06-C02-U001`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C02-U004` | **Definition — sheaf**                                                                          | §1 Sheaves; pp. 60–68; L1658–1661                         | A sheaf is a presheaf satisfying locality and gluing: a section locally zero is zero, and compatible local sections on an open cover glue uniquely.                                                                                                                                 | `FC06-C02-U001`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C02-U005` | **Example 1.0.1 — sheaf of regular functions**                                                  | §1 Sheaves; pp. 60–68; L1665                              | For a variety X, regular functions on open subsets with ordinary restriction maps form a sheaf of rings O_X.                                                                                                                                                                        | `FC06-C02-U004`, `FC06-C01-U049`, `FC06-C01-U051`                                   |
| `FC06-C02-U006` | **Example 1.0.2 — standard function sheaves**                                                   | §1 Sheaves; pp. 60–68; L1667                              | Continuous real-valued functions, differentiable functions on a manifold, and holomorphic functions on a complex manifold form sheaves.                                                                                                                                             | `FC06-C02-U004`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C02-U007` | **Example 1.0.3 — constant sheaf**                                                              | §1 Sheaves; pp. 60–68; L1669                              | For an abelian group A with the discrete topology, U↦continuous maps U→A defines the constant sheaf; on connected U its sections are A.                                                                                                                                             | `FC06-C02-U004`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C02-U008` | **Definition — stalk and germs**                                                                | §1 Sheaves; pp. 60–68; L1671–1673                         | The stalk F_P is the direct limit of F(U) over neighborhoods U of P; its elements are germs of local sections. For O_X on a variety, O_{X,P} is the local ring.                                                                                                                     | `FC06-C02-U001`, `FC06-C01-U055`                                                    |
| `FC06-C02-U009` | **Definition — morphism of presheaves/sheaves**                                                 | §1 Sheaves; pp. 60–68; L1675–1683                         | A morphism F→G is a family of maps on sections commuting with restriction; an isomorphism has a two-sided inverse and induces maps on all stalks.                                                                                                                                   | `FC06-C02-U001`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C02-U010` | **Proposition 1.1 — stalkwise isomorphism criterion**                                           | §1 Sheaves; pp. 60–68; L1685                              | A morphism of sheaves F→G is an isomorphism iff every induced stalk map F_P→G_P is an isomorphism.                                                                                                                                                                                  | `FC06-C02-U004`, `FC06-C02-U009`, `FC06-C02-U008`                                   |
| `FC06-C02-U011` | **Definition — presheaf kernel, cokernel, image**                                               | §1 Sheaves; pp. 60–68; L1693                              | For a morphism of presheaves φ:F→G, define ker, coker, and im sectionwise by U↦ker φ(U), coker φ(U), and im φ(U).                                                                                                                                                                   | `FC06-C02-U009`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C02-U012` | **Proposition-Definition 1.2 — associated sheaf**                                               | §1 Sheaves; pp. 60–68; L1697–1704                         | Every presheaf F has an associated sheaf F⁺ with a universal map F→F⁺ through which every map from F to a sheaf factors uniquely; stalks are unchanged and sheaves are fixed up to canonical isomorphism.                                                                           | `FC06-C02-U001`, `FC06-C02-U008`                                                    |
| `FC06-C02-U013` | **Definition — subsheaf**                                                                       | §1 Sheaves; pp. 60–68; L1706                              | A subsheaf F′⊆F is a sheaf whose section groups are subgroups compatible with F-restrictions; stalks F′_P embed in F_P.                                                                                                                                                             | `FC06-C02-U004`                                                                     |
