| `FC06-C03-U185` | **Proposition 12.1 — T^i form a δ-functor**                                                           | §12 The Semicontinuity Theorem; pp. 281–292; L7405                   | Each T^i is additive and covariant and exact in the middle; together the T^i and cohomological connecting maps form a δ-functor.                                                                                                                                         | `FC06-C03-U184`, `FC06-C03-U017`                                                    |
| `FC06-C03-U186` | **Proposition 12.2 — finite free complex representing fibre cohomology**                              | §12 The Semicontinuity Theorem; pp. 281–292; L7423–7429              | There exists a bounded-above complex L^• of finitely generated free A-modules such that T^i(M)≅h^i(L^•⊗_A M) naturally for every A-module M and all i, compatibly as δ-functors.                                                                                         | `FC06-C03-U185`, `FC06-C03-U059`, `FC06-C03-U062`                                   |
| `FC06-C03-U187` | **Lemma 12.3 — finite free replacement of a flat complex**                                            | §12 The Semicontinuity Theorem; pp. 281–292; L7447–7453              | A bounded-above complex C^• with finite cohomology admits a bounded-above finite-free complex L^• quasi-isomorphic to it; if every C^i is flat, tensoring with any module preserves the induced cohomology isomorphisms.                                                 | `FC06-C03-U186`, `FC06-C03-U009`, `FC06-C03-U119`                                   |
| `FC06-C03-U188` | **Construction — W^i of the representing complex**                                                    | §12 The Semicontinuity Theorem; pp. 281–292; L7495–7505              | For a complex N^• set W^i(N)=coker(d^{i−1}:N^{i−1}→N^i); then 0→h^i(N)→W^i(N)→N^{i+1} is exact. For §12 write W^i=W^i(L^•).                                                                                                                                              | `FC06-C03-U186`, `FC06-C03-U009`                                                    |
| `FC06-C03-U189` | **Proposition 12.4 — characterization of left exact T^i**                                             | §12 The Semicontinuity Theorem; pp. 281–292; L7507–7515              | The following are equivalent: T^i is left exact; W^i is projective over A; and there is a finite A-module Q such that T^i(M)≅Hom_A(Q,M) naturally for all M.                                                                                                             | `FC06-C03-U188`, `FC06-C03-U185`                                                    |
| `FC06-C03-U190` | **Remark 12.4.1 — finite representability is stronger than abstract Hom-representability**            | §12 The Semicontinuity Theorem; pp. 281–292; L7567                   | A left-exact functor on A-modules commuting with direct sums is Hom(Q,−) for some Q, but Q need not be finite even if the functor preserves finite generation; Proposition 12.4 supplies a finite Q here.                                                                | `FC06-C03-U189`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C03-U191` | **Proposition 12.5 — characterization of right exact T^i**                                            | §12 The Semicontinuity Theorem; pp. 281–292; L7571–7579              | There is a natural φ_M:T^i(A)⊗M→T^i(M); T^i is right exact iff φ_M is an isomorphism for every M, equivalently iff φ_M is surjective for every M.                                                                                                                        | `FC06-C03-U184`, `FC06-C03-U185`                                                    |
| `FC06-C03-U192` | **Corollary 12.6 — characterization of exact T^i**                                                    | §12 The Semicontinuity Theorem; pp. 281–292; L7625                   | T^i is exact iff it is right exact and the finite A-module T^i(A) is projective.                                                                                                                                                                                         | `FC06-C03-U191`, `FC06-C03-U120`                                                    |
| `FC06-C03-U193` | **Construction — local functors T_y^i**                                                               | §12 The Semicontinuity Theorem; pp. 281–292; L7632                   | For y↔p∈Spec A, restrict T^i to A_p-modules, equivalently use L_p^•; flat base change identifies this with the T^i functor for X×*Y Spec O*{Y,y}→Spec O_{Y,y}.                                                                                                           | `FC06-C03-U186`, `FC06-C03-U126`                                                    |
| `FC06-C03-U194` | **Proposition 12.7 — exactness properties are open on the base**                                      | §12 The Semicontinuity Theorem; pp. 281–292; L7634                   | If T^i is left exact, right exact, or exact at y_0∈Y, the same property holds at every point in some open neighborhood of y_0.                                                                                                                                           | `FC06-C03-U193`, `FC06-C03-U189`, `FC06-C03-U191`, `FC06-C03-U192`                  |
| `FC06-C03-U195` | **Definition — upper semicontinuous integer-valued function**                                         | §12 The Semicontinuity Theorem; pp. 281–292; L7642                   | A function φ:Y→Z is upper semicontinuous when near every y its values are ≤φ(y); equivalently, each locus {y:φ(y)≥n} is closed.                                                                                                                                          | —                                                                                   |
| `FC06-C03-U196` | **Remark 12.7.1 — closed-level-set criterion for upper semicontinuity**                               | §12 The Semicontinuity Theorem; pp. 281–292; L7644                   | An integer-valued function is upper semicontinuous iff every superlevel set {y∈Y:φ(y)≥n} is closed.                                                                                                                                                                      | `FC06-C03-U195`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C03-U197` | **Example 12.7.2 — fibre dimension of a coherent sheaf is upper semicontinuous**                      | §12 The Semicontinuity Theorem; pp. 281–292; L7646                   | For coherent F on a noetherian scheme Y, y↦dim_{k(y)}(F⊗k(y)), equivalently the minimal number of generators of F_y, is upper semicontinuous.                                                                                                                            | `FC06-C03-U195`, `FC06-C02-U111`                                                    |
| `FC06-C03-U198` | **Theorem 12.8 — semicontinuity theorem**                                                             | §12 The Semicontinuity Theorem; pp. 281–292; L7654–7660              | For projective f:X→Y of noetherian schemes and coherent F flat over Y, h^i(y,F)=dim_{k(y)}H^i(X_y,F_y) is upper semicontinuous on Y for every i≥0.                                                                                                                       | `FC06-C03-U184`, `FC06-C03-U186`, `FC06-C03-U197`, `FC06-C03-U128`                  |
| `FC06-C03-U199` | **Corollary 12.9 — Grauert theorem under constant fibre cohomology**                                  | §12 The Semicontinuity Theorem; pp. 281–292; L7694–7708              | If additionally Y is integral and h^i(y,F) is constant, then R^i f_*F is locally free and the natural map R^i f_*F⊗k(y)→H^i(X_y,F_y) is an isomorphism for every y.                                                                                                      | `FC06-C03-U198`, `FC06-C03-U192`, `FC06-C03-U114`                                   |
| `FC06-C03-U200` | **Example 12.9.1 — flat integral curves have constant h^0 and h^1**                                   | §12 The Semicontinuity Theorem; pp. 281–292; L7710                   | For a flat family of integral projective curves over algebraically closed k, h^0(O)=1 and constancy of arithmetic genus forces h^1(O) to be constant as well.                                                                                                            | `FC06-C03-U199`, `FC06-C03-U143`                                                    |
| `FC06-C03-U201` | **Example 12.9.2 — fibre cohomology jumps at an embedded-point degeneration**                         | §12 The Semicontinuity Theorem; pp. 281–292; L7712                   | In Example 9.8.4, both h^0 and h^1 of O jump upward at the special fibre because of the embedded nilpotent structure and the cubic reduced support.                                                                                                                      | `FC06-C03-U198`, `FC06-C03-U140`                                                    |
| `FC06-C03-U202` | **Example 12.9.3 — constancy in smooth complex projective families**                                  | §12 The Semicontinuity Theorem; pp. 281–292; L7714                   | For an algebraic family of nonsingular projective varieties over C, all h^i(O_{X_t}) are constant; Hartshorne notes this uses degeneration of the Hodge spectral sequence.                                                                                               | `FC06-C03-U198`                                                                     |
| `FC06-C03-U203` | **Proposition 12.10 — fibrewise surjectivity implies local right exactness**                          | §12 The Semicontinuity Theorem; pp. 281–292; L7724–7730              | If for some i,y the natural T^i(A)⊗k(y)→T^i(k(y)) is surjective, then T^i is right exact at y; the converse follows from Proposition 12.5.                                                                                                                               | `FC06-C03-U191`, `FC06-C03-U176`, `FC06-C03-U193`                                   |
| `FC06-C03-U204` | **Theorem 12.11 — cohomology and base change**                                                        | §12 The Semicontinuity Theorem; pp. 281–292; L7785–7799              | For projective f:X→Y noetherian and coherent F flat over Y: if φ^i(y):R^i f_*F⊗k(y)→H^i(X_y,F_y) is surjective, it is an isomorphism and remains so near y; assuming this, φ^{i−1}(y) is surjective iff R^i f_*F is locally free near y.                                 | `FC06-C03-U203`, `FC06-C03-U194`, `FC06-C03-U192`, `FC06-C03-U191`                  |

## Hartshorne, *Algebraic Geometry* — Chapter IV: Curves

| ID              | Kind / source unit                                                                       | Location                                                    | Concise data                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               | Earlier FC06 dependencies                                          |
| --------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| `FC06-C04-U001` | **Convention — curve in Chapter IV**                                                     | §1 Riemann–Roch; pp. 294–298; L7852                         | Throughout Chapter IV, a curve is a complete nonsingular curve over the fixed algebraically closed field k; it is integral, one-dimensional, proper, regular, and hence projective. “Point” means closed point unless the generic point is specified.                                      | `FC06-C02-U171`, `FC06-C02-U172`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U002` | **Construction/convention — arithmetic, geometric, and cohomological genus**             | §1 Riemann–Roch; pp. 294–298; L7856                         | For a curve X, p_a(X)=1−P_X(0) and p_g(X)=dim_k Γ(X,ω_X); both will be identified with h^1(X,O_X) and denoted g.                                                                                                                                                                           | `FC06-C03-U099`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U003` | **Proposition 1.1 — equality of curve genera**                                           | §1 Riemann–Roch; pp. 294–298; L7858                         | For a curve X, p_a(X)=p_g(X)=dim_k H^1(X,O_X); this common nonnegative integer is the genus g(X).                                                                                                                                                                                          | `FC06-C04-U002`, `FC06-C03-U099`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U004` | **Remark 1.1.1 — existence in every genus**                                              | §1 Riemann–Roch; pp. 294–298; L7868                         | The genus is nonnegative and every integer g≥0 occurs; for example, a nonsingular divisor of type (g+1,2) on a smooth quadric has genus g.                                                                                                                                                 | `FC06-C04-U003`, `FC06-C02-U167`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U005` | **Convention/construction — divisors, degree, class group, and Picard group on a curve** | §1 Riemann–Roch; pp. 294–298; L7870                         | On a curve every divisor is a finite sum of closed points, degree is the coefficient sum, principal divisors have degree zero, and Cl X≅Pic X; Pic^0 X is the subgroup of degree-zero invertible sheaves.                                                                                  | `FC06-C02-U174`, `FC06-C02-U177`, `FC06-C02-U196`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U006` | **Construction — l(D) and dimension of a complete linear system**                        | §1 Riemann–Roch; pp. 294–298; L7872–7878                    | For a divisor D, l(D)=dim_k H^0(X,L(D)); if l(D)>0 then \|D\| is the projective space of nonzero sections modulo scalars and dim\|D\|=l(D)−1.                                                                                                                                              | `FC06-C02-U219`, `FC06-C02-U191`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U007` | **Lemma 1.2 — nonempty degree-zero linear systems**                                      | §1 Riemann–Roch; pp. 294–298; L7882                         | If l(D)≠0 then deg D≥0; if moreover deg D=0, then D is linearly equivalent to zero.                                                                                                                                                                                                        | `FC06-C04-U006`, `FC06-C04-U005`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U008` | **Definition/construction — canonical divisor on a curve**                               | §1 Riemann–Roch; pp. 294–298; L7886                         | Since ω_X=Ω_X is invertible, any divisor K with L(K)≅ω_X is a canonical divisor; canonical divisors form the canonical linear-equivalence class.                                                                                                                                           | `FC06-C02-U280`, `FC06-C02-U191`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U009` | **Theorem 1.3 — Riemann–Roch**                                                           | §1 Riemann–Roch; pp. 294–298; L7888–7934                    | For every divisor D on a genus-g curve with canonical divisor K, l(D)−l(K−D)=deg D+1−g.                                                                                                                                                                                                    | `FC06-C04-U003`, `FC06-C04-U008`, `FC06-C04-U006`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U010` | **Remark 1.3.1 — Riemann–Roch recovers the curve Hilbert polynomial**                    | §1 Riemann–Roch; pp. 294–298; L7936                         | If X⊂P^n has degree d and D is a hyperplane section, then for n≫0 Riemann–Roch gives χ(L(nD))=nd+1−g, identifying the constant term with 1−p_a.                                                                                                                                            | `FC06-C04-U009`, `FC06-C02-U091`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U011` | **Remark 1.3.2 — asymptotic dimensions of \|nD\|**                                       | §1 Riemann–Roch; pp. 294–298; L7944                         | If deg D>0, then nD is nonspecial for n≫0 and dim\|nD\|=n deg D−g; if deg D<0 no positive multiple is effective, while degree zero is governed by torsion/triviality in Pic^0.                                                                                                             | `FC06-C04-U009`, `FC06-C04-U007`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U012` | **Example 1.3.3 — degree of the canonical divisor**                                      | §1 Riemann–Roch; pp. 294–298; L7950–7956                    | For a genus-g curve, deg K=2g−2.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           | `FC06-C04-U009`, `FC06-C04-U008`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U013` | **Example 1.3.4 — special divisors and index of speciality**                             | §1 Riemann–Roch; pp. 294–298; L7958                         | D is special when l(K−D)>0; its index of speciality is l(K−D). Every divisor of degree >2g−2 is nonspecial.                                                                                                                                                                                | `FC06-C04-U009`, `FC06-C04-U012`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U014` | **Example 1.3.5 — genus zero iff rational**                                              | §1 Riemann–Roch; pp. 294–298; L7960                         | A curve has genus 0 iff it is isomorphic to P^1.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           | `FC06-C04-U009`, `FC06-C04-U007`, `FC06-C02-U172`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U015` | **Example 1.3.6 — elliptic curves**                                                      | §1 Riemann–Roch; pp. 294–298; L7962                         | A curve has genus 1 iff its canonical class is trivial; a genus-one curve is called an elliptic curve.                                                                                                                                                                                     | `FC06-C04-U012`, `FC06-C04-U007`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U016` | **Example 1.3.7 — elliptic curve group law via Pic^0**                                   | §1 Riemann–Roch; pp. 294–298; L7964–7972                    | For elliptic X and chosen P_0, P↦L(P−P_0) is a bijection from closed points of X to Pic^0 X, transporting the Picard-group law to a group law on X with identity P_0.                                                                                                                      | `FC06-C04-U015`, `FC06-C04-U005`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U017` | **Convention — degree of a finite curve morphism**                                       | §2 Hurwitz’s Theorem; pp. 299–306; L8019                    | For a finite morphism f:X→Y of curves, deg f=[K(X):K(Y)].                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  | `FC06-C02-U174`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U018` | **Definition/construction — ramification and branch points**                             | §2 Hurwitz’s Theorem; pp. 299–306; L8021–8032               | For P∈X, Q=f(P), and a local parameter t at Q, e_P=v_P(t) is the ramification index; P is ramified if e_P>1 and Q is then a branch point.                                                                                                                                                  | `FC06-C02-U154`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U019` | **Definition — tame and wild ramification**                                              | §2 Hurwitz’s Theorem; pp. 299–306; L8032                    | Ramification at P is tame when char k does not divide e_P, and wild otherwise.                                                                                                                                                                                                             | `FC06-C04-U018`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U020` | **Construction — pullback of divisors along finite curve maps**                          | §2 Hurwitz’s Theorem; pp. 299–306; L8034–8040               | For a closed point Q of Y, f^*Q=Σ_{P↦Q}e_P P; this extends linearly to divisors, is compatible with principal divisors, and hence with pullback on Picard groups.                                                                                                                          | `FC06-C04-U018`, `FC06-C02-U175`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U021` | **Definition — separable morphism of curves**                                            | §2 Hurwitz’s Theorem; pp. 299–306; L8042                    | A finite curve morphism is separable when the induced extension K(X)/K(Y) is separable.                                                                                                                                                                                                    | `FC06-C04-U017`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U022` | **Proposition 2.1 — differential exact sequence for separable finite curve maps**        | §2 Hurwitz’s Theorem; pp. 299–306; L8044                    | If f:X→Y is finite and separable, then 0→f^*Ω_Y→Ω_X→Ω_{X/Y}→0 is exact.                                                                                                                                                                                                                    | `FC06-C04-U021`, `FC06-C02-U269`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U023` | **Notation/construction — derivative dt/du**                                             | §2 Hurwitz’s Theorem; pp. 299–306; L8052                    | For local parameters t at Q=f(P) and u at P, write f^*dt=(dt/du)du in Ω_{X,P}.                                                                                                                                                                                                             | `FC06-C04-U022`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U024` | **Proposition 2.2 — relative differentials measure ramification**                        | §2 Hurwitz’s Theorem; pp. 299–306; L8054–8078               | For finite separable f:X→Y, Ω_{X/Y} is torsion supported at finitely many ramification points; its stalk at P has length v_P(dt/du). In tame ramification this length is e_P−1, while in wild ramification it is >e_P−1.                                                                   | `FC06-C04-U023`, `FC06-C04-U018`, `FC06-C04-U019`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U025` | **Definition — ramification divisor**                                                    | §2 Hurwitz’s Theorem; pp. 299–306; L8080                    | For finite separable f:X→Y, R=Σ_P length_{O_P}(Ω_{X/Y,P})P is the effective ramification divisor.                                                                                                                                                                                          | `FC06-C04-U024`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U026` | **Proposition 2.3 — canonical divisor under a finite separable map**                     | §2 Hurwitz’s Theorem; pp. 299–306; L8086–8104               | For finite separable f:X→Y, K_X is linearly equivalent to f^*K_Y+R.                                                                                                                                                                                                                        | `FC06-C04-U025`, `FC06-C04-U008`, `FC06-C04-U020`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U027` | **Corollary 2.4 — Hurwitz formula**                                                      | §2 Hurwitz’s Theorem; pp. 299–306; L8106–8120               | If f:X→Y is finite separable of degree n, then 2g(X)−2=n(2g(Y)−2)+deg R. In the tame case deg R=Σ_P(e_P−1).                                                                                                                                                                                | `FC06-C04-U026`, `FC06-C04-U012`, `FC06-C02-U176`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U028` | **Definition — absolute Frobenius**                                                      | §2 Hurwitz’s Theorem; pp. 299–306; L8122                    | In characteristic p>0, the Frobenius morphism F:X→X is the identity on the underlying space and sends local functions to their pth powers.                                                                                                                                                 | `FC06-C02-U031`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U029` | **Remark 2.4.1 — k-linear Frobenius twist**                                              | §2 Hurwitz’s Theorem; pp. 299–306; L8124–8132               | Absolute Frobenius need not be a k-morphism. Giving X the structure map composed with Frobenius of Spec k produces X_p and a k-linear Frobenius F′:X_p→X.                                                                                                                                  | `FC06-C04-U028`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U030` | **Example 2.4.2 — Frobenius twists**                                                     | §2 Hurwitz’s Theorem; pp. 299–306; L8134                    | X_p need not be k-isomorphic to X in general; over perfect k, standard affine-space examples are isomorphic after twisting coefficients.                                                                                                                                                   | `FC06-C04-U029`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U031` | **Example 2.4.3 — Frobenius of a curve**                                                 | §2 Hurwitz’s Theorem; pp. 299–306; L8136                    | For a curve over algebraically closed characteristic-p k, the k-linear Frobenius is finite of degree p and induces the purely inseparable extension K(X) over K(X)^p.                                                                                                                      | `FC06-C04-U029`, `FC06-C04-U017`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U032` | **Proposition 2.5 — purely inseparable maps factor through Frobenius**                   | §2 Hurwitz’s Theorem; pp. 299–306; L8138–8146               | Every finite purely inseparable morphism of curves factors as a composition of k-linear Frobenius morphisms; source and target are abstractly isomorphic and have the same genus.                                                                                                          | `FC06-C04-U031`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U033` | **Example 2.5.1 — Frobenius is everywhere ramified**                                     | §2 Hurwitz’s Theorem; pp. 299–306; L8148                    | The k-linear Frobenius on a curve has e_P=p everywhere; its differential pullback is zero and Ω_{X/X_p} identifies with Ω_X.                                                                                                                                                               | `FC06-C04-U032`, `FC06-C04-U018`, `FC06-C02-U265`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U034` | **Example 2.5.2 — even ramification degree**                                             | §2 Hurwitz’s Theorem; pp. 299–306; L8150                    | For any finite separable morphism of curves, deg R is even by Hurwitz.                                                                                                                                                                                                                     | `FC06-C04-U027`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U035` | **Example 2.5.3 — étale coverings and simple connectedness**                             | §2 Hurwitz’s Theorem; pp. 299–306; L8152–8160               | A finite separable curve morphism is étale when unramified. Trivial covers are disjoint unions of copies; a curve is simply connected if it has no nontrivial connected étale cover. P^1 is simply connected.                                                                              | `FC06-C04-U027`, `FC06-C04-U025`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U036` | **Example 2.5.4 — genus under finite maps**                                              | §2 Hurwitz’s Theorem; pp. 299–306; L8162                    | For any finite morphism X→Y of curves, g(X)≥g(Y). In the separable case equality can occur only for degree one, or for g(Y)=1 with an unramified map.                                                                                                                                      | `FC06-C04-U027`, `FC06-C04-U032`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U037` | **Example 2.5.5 — Lüroth theorem**                                                       | §2 Hurwitz’s Theorem; pp. 299–306; L8170–8172               | If k⊂L⊂k(t) with trdeg_k L=1, then L≅k(u): the corresponding finite map P^1→Y forces g(Y)=0.                                                                                                                                                                                               | `FC06-C04-U036`, `FC06-C04-U014`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U038` | **Convention — ample and very ample divisors on curves**                                 | §3 Embeddings in Projective Space; pp. 307–315; L8272       | A divisor D is ample or very ample when L(D) has that property; \|D\| is the complete linear system and a base point is a point common to every effective divisor in \|D\|.                                                                                                                | `FC06-C02-U207`, `FC06-C02-U140`, `FC06-C02-U219`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U039` | **Proposition 3.1 — linear-system criteria for basepoint freeness and very ampleness**   | §3 Embeddings in Projective Space; pp. 307–315; L8278–8332  | For a divisor D: \|D\| is basepoint-free iff dim\|D−P\|=dim\|D\|−1 for every P; D is very ample iff dim\|D−P−Q\|=dim\|D\|−2 for all P,Q, including P=Q.                                                                                                                                    | `FC06-C04-U038`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U040` | **Corollary 3.2 — degree bounds for global generation and very ampleness**               | §3 Embeddings in Projective Space; pp. 307–315; L8334–8339  | On a genus-g curve, deg D≥2g implies \|D\| is basepoint-free, and deg D≥2g+1 implies D is very ample.                                                                                                                                                                                      | `FC06-C04-U039`, `FC06-C04-U009`, `FC06-C04-U012`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U041` | **Corollary 3.3 — ampleness is positive degree**                                         | §3 Embeddings in Projective Space; pp. 307–315; L8341       | A divisor on a curve is ample iff it has positive degree.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  | `FC06-C04-U040`, `FC06-C02-U212`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U042` | **Example 3.3.1 — positivity on P^1**                                                    | §3 Embeddings in Projective Space; pp. 307–315; L8345       | On a genus-zero curve, D is ample iff very ample iff deg D>0.                                                                                                                                                                                                                              | `FC06-C04-U014`, `FC06-C04-U041`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U043` | **Example 3.3.2 — degree of an embedding**                                               | §3 Embeddings in Projective Space; pp. 307–315; L8348       | If a very ample divisor D embeds X into projective space by its complete system, the degree of the embedded curve is deg D.                                                                                                                                                                | `FC06-C04-U038`, `FC06-C02-U091`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U044` | **Example 3.3.3 — elliptic plane cubics**                                                | §3 Embeddings in Projective Space; pp. 307–315; L8350–8352  | Every degree-three divisor on an elliptic curve is very ample and embeds it as a plane cubic; on an elliptic curve, very ample is equivalent to degree at least three.                                                                                                                     | `FC06-C04-U015`, `FC06-C04-U040`, `FC06-C04-U043`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U045` | **Example 3.3.4 — genus-two degree-five embedding**                                      | §3 Embeddings in Projective Space; pp. 307–315; L8354       | Every degree-five divisor on a genus-two curve is very ample, giving a degree-five embedding in P^3.                                                                                                                                                                                       | `FC06-C04-U040`, `FC06-C04-U043`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U046` | **Example 3.3.5 — the 2g+1 bound need not be sharp**                                     | §3 Embeddings in Projective Space; pp. 307–315; L8356       | A nonsingular plane quartic has genus three and its degree-four hyperplane divisor is already very ample.                                                                                                                                                                                  | `FC06-C04-U040`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U047` | **Definition/construction — secant and tangent lines**                                   | §3 Embeddings in Projective Space; pp. 307–315; L8360       | For X⊂P^n, a secant line contains two distinct points of X; a tangent line meets X with multiplicity at least two at a point.                                                                                                                                                              | `FC06-C02-U091`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U048` | **Proposition 3.4 — projection from a point preserves an embedding**                     | §3 Embeddings in Projective Space; pp. 307–315; L8362–8366  | For O∉X, projection from O gives a closed immersion X→P^{n−1} iff O lies on neither a secant nor a tangent line of X.                                                                                                                                                                      | `FC06-C04-U047`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U049` | **Proposition 3.5 — project curves down from P^n**                                       | §3 Embeddings in Projective Space; pp. 307–315; L8368–8374  | If X⊂P^n with n≥4, there exists O∉X such that projection from O is a closed immersion into P^{n−1}; the secant and tangent loci have dimension at most 3 and 2 respectively.                                                                                                               | `FC06-C04-U048`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U050` | **Corollary 3.6 — every curve embeds in P^3**                                            | §3 Embeddings in Projective Space; pp. 307–315; L8376       | Every curve admits a closed immersion into P^3.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            | `FC06-C04-U049`, `FC06-C04-U040`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U051` | **Definition/construction — multisecants and coplanar tangent secants**                  | §3 Embeddings in Projective Space; pp. 307–315; L8382       | A multisecant meets X in at least three points; a secant through P,Q has coplanar tangents when the tangent lines at P and Q lie in one plane.                                                                                                                                             | `FC06-C04-U047`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U052` | **Proposition 3.7 — projection to a nodal plane curve**                                  | §3 Embeddings in Projective Space; pp. 307–315; L8384–8390  | For X⊂P^3 and O∉X, projection to P^2 is birational with image having only nodes iff only finitely many secants pass through O, O lies on no tangent or multisecant, and no secant through O has coplanar tangents.                                                                         | `FC06-C04-U051`, `FC06-C04-U048`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U053` | **Proposition 3.8 — degeneracy of secants forces a common tangent point**                | §3 Embeddings in Projective Space; pp. 307–315; L8392–8398  | If every secant of a projective curve is a multisecant, or if every secant has coplanar tangent lines, then there is a point A lying on every tangent line to X.                                                                                                                           | `FC06-C04-U051`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U054` | **Definition — strange curve**                                                           | §3 Embeddings in Projective Space; pp. 307–315; L8400       | A projective curve is strange if all its tangent lines pass through one point A.                                                                                                                                                                                                           | `FC06-C04-U053`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U055` | **Example 3.8.1 — lines are strange**                                                    | §3 Embeddings in Projective Space; pp. 307–315; L8402       | A line is strange.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         | `FC06-C04-U054`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U056` | **Example 3.8.2 — characteristic-two conics are strange**                                | §3 Embeddings in Projective Space; pp. 307–315; L8404       | In characteristic two, a nonsingular plane conic is strange.                                                                                                                                                                                                                               | `FC06-C04-U054`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U057` | **Theorem 3.9 — Samuel classification of strange curves**                                | §3 Embeddings in Projective Space; pp. 307–315; L8406–8468  | The only strange curves are lines and, in characteristic two, nonsingular conics.                                                                                                                                                                                                          | `FC06-C04-U054`, `FC06-C04-U055`, `FC06-C04-U056`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U058` | **Theorem 3.10 — nodal plane model**                                                     | §3 Embeddings in Projective Space; pp. 307–315; L8470–8474  | Every curve embedded in P^3 admits projection from a suitable point to a plane curve such that the projection is birational and the only singularities of the image are nodes.                                                                                                             | `FC06-C04-U052`, `FC06-C04-U057`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U059` | **Corollary 3.11 — every curve is birational to a nodal plane curve**                    | §3 Embeddings in Projective Space; pp. 307–315; L8476       | Every curve is birationally equivalent to a plane curve having at most nodes.                                                                                                                                                                                                              | `FC06-C04-U050`, `FC06-C04-U058`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U060` | **Remark 3.11.1 — Severi varieties of nodal plane curves**                               | §3 Embeddings in Projective Space; pp. 307–315; L8480–8494  | A degree-d plane curve with r nodes has normalization genus g=(d−1)(d−2)/2−r. Thus 0≤r≤(d−1)(d−2)/2; both extremes occur, and the corresponding Severi loci organize plane curves by geometric genus.                                                                                      | `FC06-C04-U059`, `FC06-C04-U003`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U061` | **Convention — characteristic in the elliptic-curve section**                            | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8550                      | Except where stated otherwise, §4 omits characteristic 2; most results persist there with additional care.                                                                                                                                                                                 | `FC06-C04-U015`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U062` | **Definition/construction — Legendre parameter and j-invariant**                         | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8556–8564                 | For elliptic X and P_0 in characteristic ≠2, \|2P_0\| gives a separable degree-two map to P^1 ramified over four points. After moving them to 0,1,λ,∞ with λ≠0,1, define j=2^8(λ^2−λ+1)^3/(λ^2(λ−1)^2).                                                                                    | `FC06-C04-U015`, `FC06-C04-U027`, `FC06-C04-U039`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U063` | **Theorem 4.1 — j classifies elliptic curves**                                           | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8566                      | Over an algebraically closed field of characteristic ≠2, j depends only on X; two elliptic curves are isomorphic iff their j-invariants agree; and every j∈k occurs.                                                                                                                       | `FC06-C04-U062`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U064` | **Lemma 4.2 — involution associated to two points**                                      | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8572                      | For any P,Q on an elliptic curve, there is an involution σ with σ(P)=Q and R+σ(R)∼P+Q for every R.                                                                                                                                                                                         | `FC06-C04-U015`, `FC06-C04-U009`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U065` | **Corollary 4.3 — automorphisms act transitively**                                       | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8576                      | The automorphism group of an elliptic curve acts transitively on its points.                                                                                                                                                                                                               | `FC06-C04-U064`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U066` | **Lemma 4.4 — uniqueness of degree-two maps up to automorphisms**                        | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8578                      | Any two degree-two morphisms X→P^1 from an elliptic curve differ by an automorphism of X and an automorphism of P^1.                                                                                                                                                                       | `FC06-C04-U064`, `FC06-C04-U021`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U067` | **Lemma 4.5 — six cross-ratio values**                                                   | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8586–8592                 | Permuting four points on P^1 sends the Legendre parameter λ through the six values λ,1/λ,1−λ,1/(1−λ),λ/(λ−1),(λ−1)/λ, with coincidences only in the exceptional cases described by characteristic and λ.                                                                                   | `FC06-C04-U062`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U068` | **Proposition 4.6 — Legendre normal form**                                               | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8594–8672                 | For elliptic X over algebraically closed char≠2 with chosen P_0, there is a closed immersion X→P^2 taking P_0 to (0,1,0) and giving an equation y^2z=x(x−z)(x−λz) for some λ≠0,1.                                                                                                          | `FC06-C04-U066`, `FC06-C04-U044`, `FC06-C04-U062`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U069` | **Example 4.6.1 — y²=x³−x has j=1728**                                                   | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8674                      | The nonsingular curve y²=x³−x has λ=−1 and j=2^6·3^3=1728 in characteristic ≠2.                                                                                                                                                                                                            | `FC06-C04-U068`, `FC06-C04-U062`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U070` | **Example 4.6.2 — Fermat cubic has j=0**                                                 | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8676–8682                 | The Fermat cubic x³+y³=z³ is nonsingular in characteristic ≠3; after a change to Legendre/Weierstrass form its parameter is −ω or −ω², so j=0.                                                                                                                                             | `FC06-C04-U068`, `FC06-C04-U062`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U071` | **Corollary 4.7 — automorphisms fixing the origin**                                      | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8684–8696                 | For char k≠2 and G=Aut(X,P_0): \|G\|=2 if j≠0,1728; 4 if j=1728 and char≠3; 6 if j=0 and char≠3; and 12 when char=3 and j=0=1728.                                                                                                                                                          | `FC06-C04-U067`, `FC06-C04-U069`, `FC06-C04-U070`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U072` | **Construction — group law from Pic^0**                                                  | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8700–8702                 | With base point P_0, the bijection P↦L(P−P_0) transports Pic^0X to a group law characterized by P+Q=R iff P+Q∼R+P_0; in the \|3P_0\| plane embedding, three collinear points sum to zero.                                                                                                  | `FC06-C04-U016`, `FC06-C04-U044`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U073` | **Proposition 4.8 — elliptic group operations are morphisms**                            | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8706–8714                 | For the group law on (X,P_0), inversion ρ:P↦−P and addition μ:X×X→X are morphisms, so X is a group variety.                                                                                                                                                                                | `FC06-C04-U072`, `FC06-C04-U064`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U074` | **Example 4.8.1 — multiplication-by-n morphisms**                                        | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8716                      | For every integer n, iterated group addition gives [n]:X→X. For n≠0 it will be finite of degree n²; prime-to-characteristic n-torsion is (Z/nZ)^2, while p-torsion depends on the Hasse invariant.                                                                                         | `FC06-C04-U073`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U075` | **Example 4.8.2 — two-torsion**                                                          | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8718                      | [2] is finite; in characteristic ≠2 it has degree four and kernel Z/2Z×Z/2Z, the four ramification points of \|2P_0\|.                                                                                                                                                                     | `FC06-C04-U074`, `FC06-C04-U062`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U076` | **Example 4.8.3 — three-torsion and flexes**                                             | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8720                      | In characteristic ≠2,3, [3] has degree nine and kernel (Z/3Z)^2; its points are exactly the nine inflection points of the \|3P_0\| cubic embedding.                                                                                                                                        | `FC06-C04-U074`, `FC06-C04-U072`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U077` | **Lemma 4.9 — basepoint-preserving maps are homomorphisms**                              | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8722                      | A morphism f:(X,P_0)→(X′,P′_0) of elliptic curves sending P_0 to P′_0 is automatically a homomorphism of the group structures.                                                                                                                                                             | `FC06-C04-U072`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U078` | **Definition — endomorphism ring of a pointed elliptic curve**                           | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8726                      | End(X,P_0) consists of self-morphisms fixing P_0, with pointwise addition via μ and multiplication by composition; zero is the constant P_0 map and one is the identity.                                                                                                                   | `FC06-C04-U077`, `FC06-C04-U073`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U079` | **Proposition 4.10 — integers embed in the endomorphism ring**                           | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8728–8732                 | If char k≠2, n↦[n] is an injective ring homomorphism Z→End(X,P_0); in particular [n] is finite for every n≠0.                                                                                                                                                                              | `FC06-C04-U078`, `FC06-C04-U075`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U080` | **Remark 4.10.1 — units and exceptional j-values**                                       | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8734                      | The unit group of End(X,P_0) is Aut(X,P_0); for j=0 or 1728 it is larger than {±1}, so the endomorphism ring is strictly larger than Z.                                                                                                                                                    | `FC06-C04-U079`, `FC06-C04-U071`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U081` | **Construction — relative degree-zero Picard functor**                                   | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8740                      | For a curve X and finite-type k-scheme T, Pic^0(X/T) is the subgroup of Pic(X×T) restricting to degree zero on fibres, modulo line bundles pulled back from T.                                                                                                                             | `FC06-C04-U005`, `FC06-C02-U070`, `FC06-C02-U106`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U082` | **Definition — Jacobian variety**                                                        | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8742                      | A Jacobian of X is a finite-type k-scheme J with a universal class L∈Pic^0(X/J) representing the functor T↦Pic^0(X/T).                                                                                                                                                                     | `FC06-C04-U081`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U083` | **Remark 4.10.2 — representable-functor formulation**                                    | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8744                      | The Jacobian is precisely a representing object for the relative Picard functor T↦Pic^0(X/T).                                                                                                                                                                                              | `FC06-C04-U082`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U084` | **Remark 4.10.3 — uniqueness and existence**                                             | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8746                      | The universal property makes J unique when it exists; for elliptic X one can take J=X, while higher-genus existence is substantially deeper.                                                                                                                                               | `FC06-C04-U082`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U085` | **Remark 4.10.4 — closed points of J are Pic^0 classes**                                 | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8748                      | Assuming J exists, its closed points are naturally in bijection with Pic^0X.                                                                                                                                                                                                               | `FC06-C04-U082`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U086` | **Definition — group scheme**                                                            | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8750–8753                 | A group scheme X/S has identity section e, inverse ρ, and multiplication μ:X×_S X→X satisfying the unit/inverse and associativity identities as morphisms.                                                                                                                                 | `FC06-C02-U045`, `FC06-C02-U070`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U087` | **Remark 4.10.5 — group schemes generalize group varieties**                             | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8755                      | Over Spec k, a group scheme whose total space is a variety recovers the earlier notion of group variety; the morphism identities induce the usual pointwise group axioms.                                                                                                                  | `FC06-C04-U086`, `FC06-C04-U073`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U088` | **Remark 4.10.6 — the Jacobian is canonically a group scheme**                           | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8757                      | The representing property defines identity, inverse, and addition on J from 0, inverse line bundles, and tensor product, making the Jacobian a group scheme.                                                                                                                               | `FC06-C04-U082`, `FC06-C04-U086`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U089` | **Remark 4.10.7 — tangent space of the Jacobian**                                        | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8759                      | The Zariski tangent space T_0J is naturally H^1(X,O_X), via degree-zero line bundles over k[ε]/(ε²).                                                                                                                                                                                       | `FC06-C04-U082`, `FC06-C04-U003`, `FC06-C03-U149`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U090` | **Remark 4.10.8 — the Jacobian is proper**                                               | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8761                      | The valuative criterion shows J is proper: an invertible sheaf on X×Spec K over a DVR extends uniquely across X×Spec R.                                                                                                                                                                    | `FC06-C04-U082`, `FC06-C02-U089`, `FC06-C02-U184`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U091` | **Remark 4.10.9 — Abel–Jacobi maps and dimension of J**                                  | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8763–8771                 | Fixing P_0 defines φ_n:X^n→J by (P_1,…,P_n)↦L(P_1+⋯+P_n−nP_0). It is surjective for n≥g; general fibres of φ_g are finite, so J is irreducible of dimension g and nonsingular.                                                                                                             | `FC06-C04-U082`, `FC06-C04-U009`, `FC06-C04-U089`, `FC06-C03-U167` |
| `FC06-C04-U092` | **Theorem 4.11 — an elliptic curve is its own Jacobian**                                 | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8773–8791                 | For elliptic X with P_0, take J=X and the universal class L(Δ)⊗p_1^*L(−P_0) on X×J, where Δ is the diagonal. This represents Pic^0(X/−), and its induced group structure agrees with the earlier elliptic group law.                                                                       | `FC06-C04-U082`, `FC06-C04-U072`, `FC06-C04-U081`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U093` | **Definition — elliptic function**                                                       | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8800                      | For a lattice Λ=Z+Zτ⊂C with τ∉R, an elliptic function is a meromorphic f(z) satisfying f(z+ω)=f(z) for all ω∈Λ.                                                                                                                                                                            | —                                                                  |
| `FC06-C04-U094` | **Construction — Weierstrass ℘ and derivative**                                          | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8804–8816                 | The lattice sum ℘(z)=1/z²+Σ_{ω∈Λ{0}}((z−ω)^{-2}−ω^{-2}) defines an elliptic function with double poles at Λ; its derivative ℘′ is also elliptic.                                                                                                                                           | `FC06-C04-U093`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U095` | **Theorem 4.12B — field of elliptic functions**                                          | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8820–8832                 | The field of elliptic functions for Λ is C(℘,℘′), with relation (℘′)²=4℘³−g_2℘−g_3 where g_2=60Σω^{-4} and g_3=140Σω^{-6}.                                                                                                                                                                 | `FC06-C04-U094`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U096` | **Construction/comparison — C/Λ as an elliptic curve**                                   | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8834–8840                 | z↦(℘(z),℘′(z)) identifies C/Λ biholomorphically with the nonsingular cubic y²=4x³−g_2x−g_3, and identifies elliptic functions with its rational function field.                                                                                                                            | `FC06-C04-U095`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U097` | **Theorem 4.13B — divisor criterion for elliptic functions**                             | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8842                      | For distinct a_i∈C/Λ and integers n_i, there is an elliptic function with divisor Σn_i(a_i) iff Σn_i=0 and Σn_i a_i=0 in C/Λ.                                                                                                                                                              | `FC06-C04-U096`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U098` | **Comparison — analytic and algebraic group laws agree**                                 | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8846                      | Under C/Λ≅X, addition modulo Λ agrees with the Picard/divisor group law on the elliptic curve.                                                                                                                                                                                             | `FC06-C04-U097`, `FC06-C04-U072`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U099` | **Theorem 4.14B — existence of a uniformizing lattice**                                  | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8848–8854                 | For c_2,c_3∈C with Δ=c_2^3−27c_3²≠0, there exist τ∉R and α≠0 such that the lattice Z+Zτ has g_2=α^4c_2 and g_3=α^6c_3; hence every complex elliptic curve is uniformized by a lattice.                                                                                                     | `FC06-C04-U095`, `FC06-C04-U068`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U100` | **Theorem 4.15B — modular equivalence of lattices**                                      | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8856–8877                 | For τ,τ′∈C\R, J(τ)=J(τ′) iff τ′=(aτ+b)/(cτ+d) for integers a,b,c,d with ad−bc=±1; here j=1728J.                                                                                                                                                                                            | `FC06-C04-U099`, `FC06-C04-U063`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U101` | **Theorem 4.16 — topology/group structure of a complex elliptic curve**                  | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8881                      | As an abstract group, a complex elliptic curve is R/Z×R/Z; in particular its n-torsion is (Z/nZ)^2.                                                                                                                                                                                        | `FC06-C04-U098`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U102` | **Corollary 4.17 — degree of multiplication-by-n over C**                                | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8885                      | For a complex elliptic curve, [n]:X→X is finite of degree n².                                                                                                                                                                                                                              | `FC06-C04-U101`, `FC06-C04-U074`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U103` | **Proposition 4.18 — analytic description of endomorphisms**                             | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8891–8897                 | For X=C/Λ, End(X,P_0) corresponds bijectively to complex α with αΛ⊆Λ; this identifies the endomorphism ring with a subring of C.                                                                                                                                                           | `FC06-C04-U078`, `FC06-C04-U098`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U104` | **Remark 4.18.1 — [n] corresponds to scalar n**                                          | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8899                      | Under the analytic endomorphism description, [n] is multiplication by n on C, giving another proof of Z↪End(X,P_0) over C.                                                                                                                                                                 | `FC06-C04-U103`, `FC06-C04-U079`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U105` | **Definition — complex multiplication**                                                  | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8901                      | A complex elliptic curve has complex multiplication when End(X,P_0) is strictly larger than Z.                                                                                                                                                                                             | `FC06-C04-U078`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U106` | **Theorem 4.19 — complex multiplication and imaginary quadratic lattices**               | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8903–8947                 | X=C/(Z+Zτ) has complex multiplication iff τ lies in an imaginary quadratic field. Then End(X) is an order in that field; for τ=r+s√−d with r,s∈Q, Hartshorne gives the explicit order {a+bτ: a,b∈Z, 2br and b(r²+ds²) integral}.                                                           | `FC06-C04-U105`, `FC06-C04-U103`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U107` | **Corollary 4.20 — CM j-invariants are countable**                                       | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8949                      | Only countably many j∈C correspond to elliptic curves with complex multiplication.                                                                                                                                                                                                         | `FC06-C04-U106`, `FC06-C04-U063`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U108` | **Example 4.20.1 — Gaussian CM**                                                         | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8953–8959                 | For τ=i, End X=Z[i], its unit group has order four, and j=1728.                                                                                                                                                                                                                            | `FC06-C04-U106`, `FC06-C04-U071`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U109` | **Example 4.20.2 — Eisenstein CM**                                                       | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8961                      | For τ=ω with ω³=1, End X=Z[ω], its unit group has order six, and j=0.                                                                                                                                                                                                                      | `FC06-C04-U106`, `FC06-C04-U071`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U110` | **Example 4.20.3 — nonmaximal CM order**                                                 | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8963                      | For τ=2i, End X=Z[2i], the order of conductor two in Q(i), strictly smaller than Z[i].                                                                                                                                                                                                     | `FC06-C04-U106`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U111` | **Remark 4.20.4 — class-field-theoretic CM data**                                        | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8965–8967                 | If End X=R has quotient field K imaginary quadratic and j=j(X), then j is an algebraic integer, K(j)/K is abelian of degree the class number of R, and j∈Z exactly when that class number is one; there are thirteen such integral j-values.                                               | `FC06-C04-U106`, `FC06-C04-U107`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U112` | **Construction — Frobenius on H¹(O_X)**                                                  | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8971–8977                 | In characteristic p, Frobenius induces a p-linear map F^*:H^1(X,O_X)→H^1(X,O_X); for elliptic X this one-dimensional map is either zero or bijective.                                                                                                                                      | `FC06-C04-U028`, `FC06-C04-U003`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U113` | **Definition — Hasse invariant and supersingularity**                                    | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8979                      | An elliptic curve has Hasse invariant 0, or is supersingular, when F^*=0 on H^1(O_X); otherwise its Hasse invariant is 1.                                                                                                                                                                  | `FC06-C04-U112`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U114` | **Proposition 4.21 — Hasse invariant from a plane cubic equation**                       | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L8983–9007                 | For a plane cubic f(x,y,z)=0 in characteristic p, the Hasse invariant is zero iff the coefficient of (xyz)^{p−1} in f^{p−1} is zero.                                                                                                                                                       | `FC06-C04-U113`, `FC06-C03-U061`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U115` | **Corollary 4.22 — Hasse polynomial for a Legendre curve**                               | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L9009–9021                 | For p≠2 and y²=x(x−1)(x−λ), supersingularity is equivalent to h_p(λ)=0, where h_p(λ)=Σ_{i=0}^k binom(k,i)^2 λ^i and k=(p−1)/2.                                                                                                                                                             | `FC06-C04-U114`, `FC06-C04-U068`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U116` | **Corollary 4.23 — finiteness of supersingular elliptic curves**                         | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L9023–9027                 | For fixed p there are finitely many supersingular elliptic curves over the algebraic closure, at most ⌊p/12⌋+2 isomorphism classes.                                                                                                                                                        | `FC06-C04-U115`, `FC06-C04-U063`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U117` | **Example 4.23.1 — supersingular j in characteristic 3**                                 | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L9029                      | For p=3, h_p(λ)=λ+1, whose only root gives j=0=1728.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       | `FC06-C04-U115`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U118` | **Example 4.23.2 — supersingular j in characteristic 5**                                 | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L9031                      | For p=5, h_p(λ)=λ²−λ+1; its two roots correspond to j=0.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   | `FC06-C04-U115`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U119` | **Example 4.23.3 — supersingular j in characteristic 7**                                 | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L9033–9039                 | For p=7, h_p(λ)=λ³+9λ²+9λ+1 has roots −1,2,4, all corresponding to j=1728.                                                                                                                                                                                                                 | `FC06-C04-U115`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U120` | **Remark 4.23.4 — supersingular primes of a fixed elliptic curve**                       | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L9041–9057                 | For an elliptic curve over Q reduced modulo good primes: in the CM case supersingular primes have density 1/2 and are characterized by ramified/inert behavior in the CM field; in the non-CM case they have density 0 but are infinite (Elkies), with Lang–Trotter predicting ~c√x/log x. | `FC06-C04-U113`, `FC06-C04-U105`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U121` | **Example 4.23.5 — supersingular primes for y²=x³−x**                                    | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L9059–9065                 | For y²=x³−x with j=1728 and CM by i, good primes p≡1 mod4 have Hasse invariant 1 and p≡3 mod4 have Hasse invariant 0; the supersingular primes have density 1/2.                                                                                                                           | `FC06-C04-U120`, `FC06-C04-U108`, `FC06-C04-U114`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U122` | **Example 4.23.6 — sparse supersingular primes in a non-CM example**                     | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L9067                      | For y²=x(x−1)(x+2), j=2^6·3^{-2}·7^3 is nonintegral and the curve has no CM; among p≤73 only p=23 is supersingular.                                                                                                                                                                        | `FC06-C04-U120`, `FC06-C04-U115`, `FC06-C04-U111`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U123` | **Construction — rational points over a field of definition**                            | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L9071–9073                 | If a pointed plane elliptic curve (X,P_0) is defined over k_0⊆k, then the k_0-rational points form a subgroup X(k_0) of X(k).                                                                                                                                                              | `FC06-C04-U072`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U124` | **Theorem/comparison — Mordell finite generation**                                       | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L9075                      | For an elliptic curve over Q, Mordell’s theorem states that X(Q) is a finitely generated abelian group.                                                                                                                                                                                    | `FC06-C04-U123`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U125` | **Example 4.23.7 — rational points on the Fermat cubic**                                 | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L9077                      | The Fermat cubic x³+y³=z³ has exactly three Q-rational points; with any one as origin they form Z/3Z.                                                                                                                                                                                      | `FC06-C04-U123`, `FC06-C04-U070`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U126` | **Example 4.23.8 — a rank-one elliptic curve over Q**                                    | §4 Elliptic Curves; pp. 316–339; L9079                      | For y²+y=x³−x with the point at infinity as origin, X(Q) is infinite cyclic generated by (0,0).                                                                                                                                                                                            | `FC06-C04-U123`, `FC06-C04-U124`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U127` | **Lemma 5.1 — canonical system is basepoint-free**                                       | §5 The Canonical Embedding; pp. 340–348; L9220–9226         | If X has genus g≥2, the canonical linear system \|K\| has no base points.                                                                                                                                                                                                                  | `FC06-C04-U008`, `FC06-C04-U039`, `FC06-C04-U009`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U128` | **Definition — hyperelliptic curve**                                                     | §5 The Canonical Embedding; pp. 340–348; L9228              | A genus-g curve with g≥2 is hyperelliptic if it admits a degree-two morphism to P^1, equivalently if it has a g^1_2.                                                                                                                                                                       | `FC06-C04-U017`, `FC06-C04-U038`                                   |
