| `FC06-C04-U129` | **Proposition 5.2 — canonical system and hyperellipticity**                              | §5 The Canonical Embedding; pp. 340–348; L9228–9236         | For g≥2, the canonical divisor is very ample iff X is not hyperelliptic.                                                                                                                                                                                                                   | `FC06-C04-U127`, `FC06-C04-U128`, `FC06-C04-U039`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U130` | **Definition — canonical embedding and canonical curve**                                 | §5 The Canonical Embedding; pp. 340–348; L9238              | For a nonhyperelliptic curve of genus g≥3, the closed immersion given by \|K\| into P^{g−1} is the canonical embedding; its image is the canonical curve.                                                                                                                                  | `FC06-C04-U129`, `FC06-C04-U012`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U131` | **Example 5.2.1 — genus-three canonical curves**                                         | §5 The Canonical Embedding; pp. 340–348; L9240              | A nonhyperelliptic genus-three curve is canonically embedded as a nonsingular plane quartic, and conversely every nonsingular plane quartic is such a canonical curve.                                                                                                                     | `FC06-C04-U130`, `FC06-C04-U012`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U132` | **Example 5.2.2 — genus-four canonical curves**                                          | §5 The Canonical Embedding; pp. 340–348; L9242–9270         | A nonhyperelliptic genus-four canonical curve is a degree-six curve in P^3 lying on a unique irreducible quadric and is a complete intersection of that quadric with a cubic; conversely such smooth intersections are canonical genus-four curves.                                        | `FC06-C04-U130`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U133` | **Proposition 5.3 — canonical map of a hyperelliptic curve**                             | §5 The Canonical Embedding; pp. 340–348; L9272–9286         | For hyperelliptic X of genus g≥2, the g^1_2 is unique. The canonical morphism factors through the degree-two map X→P^1 followed by the (g−1)-uple embedding P^1→P^{g−1}; every effective canonical divisor is a sum of g−1 fibres of the g^1_2.                                            | `FC06-C04-U128`, `FC06-C04-U127`, `FC06-C04-U039`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U134` | **Theorem 5.4 — Clifford theorem**                                                       | §5 The Canonical Embedding; pp. 340–348; L9288–9294         | If D is an effective special divisor on a curve, then dim\|D\|≤(1/2)deg D. Equality occurs for D=0 or K, or on a hyperelliptic curve for a multiple of the unique g^1_2.                                                                                                                   | `FC06-C04-U013`, `FC06-C04-U133`, `FC06-C04-U009`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U135` | **Lemma 5.5 — dimension under addition of effective divisors**                           | §5 The Canonical Embedding; pp. 340–348; L9296–9304         | For effective divisors D,E with complete linear systems of dimensions r,s, the complete system \|D+E\| has dimension at least r+s; Hartshorne uses this repeatedly to build g^r_d systems.                                                                                                 | `FC06-C04-U006`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U136` | **Remark 5.5.1 — existence range for pencils**                                           | §5 The Canonical Embedding; pp. 340–348; L9378              | Every genus-g curve has a g^1_d for every d≥g/2+1 (with the integer interpretation implicit), while general curves need not have pencils of smaller degree; this motivates gonality.                                                                                                       | `FC06-C04-U135`, `FC06-C04-U009`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U137` | **Example 5.5.2 — genus three and four curves have g^1_3**                               | §5 The Canonical Embedding; pp. 340–348; L9380–9382         | Every curve of genus 3 or 4 has a g^1_3; for a nonhyperelliptic genus-three plane quartic it arises by projection from a point of the curve.                                                                                                                                               | `FC06-C04-U136`, `FC06-C04-U131`, `FC06-C04-U048`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U138` | **Example 5.5.3 — trigonal genus-five canonical curves**                                 | §5 The Canonical Embedding; pp. 340–348; L9384–9405         | For a nonhyperelliptic genus-five canonical curve X⊂P^4, a g^1_3 yields trisecant lines. If X has no trisecants, it is the complete intersection of three quadrics; if it is trigonal, the trisecants sweep a rational surface containing X.                                               | `FC06-C04-U130`, `FC06-C04-U136`, `FC06-C04-U047`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U139` | **Remark 5.5.4 — moduli space of curves**                                                | §5 The Canonical Embedding; pp. 340–348; L9407              | For g≥2, isomorphism classes of nonsingular projective genus-g curves form an irreducible quasi-projective coarse moduli space M_g of dimension 3g−3.                                                                                                                                      | `FC06-C04-U003`                                                    |
| `FC06-C04-U140` | **Example 5.5.5 — hyperelliptic locus in moduli**                                        | §5 The Canonical Embedding; pp. 340–348; L9409              | The hyperelliptic locus is irreducible of dimension 2g−1 inside M_g; for g=2 every curve is hyperelliptic.                                                                                                                                                                                 | `FC06-C04-U139`, `FC06-C04-U128`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U141` | **Example 5.5.6 — genus-three moduli strata**                                            | §5 The Canonical Embedding; pp. 340–348; L9411              | In M_3 the hyperelliptic locus has dimension five, while nonhyperelliptic curves are plane quartics and fill the six-dimensional moduli space.                                                                                                                                             | `FC06-C04-U139`, `FC06-C04-U140`, `FC06-C04-U131`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U142` | **Proposition 6.1 — Halphen bound for nonspecial very ample divisors**                   | §6 Classification of Curves in P^3; pp. 349–355; L9460–9476 | For a curve of genus g≥2, there exists a nonspecial very ample divisor of degree d iff d≥g+3.                                                                                                                                                                                              | `FC06-C04-U040`, `FC06-C04-U013`, `FC06-C04-U009`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U143` | **Corollary 6.2 — nonspecial curves in P^3**                                             | §6 Classification of Curves in P^3; pp. 349–355; L9478–9482 | There exists a nonsingular nonplanar curve of degree d and genus g in P^3 whose hyperplane divisor is nonspecial iff d≥g+3.                                                                                                                                                                | `FC06-C04-U142`, `FC06-C04-U049`, `FC06-C04-U043`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U144` | **Proposition 6.3 — lower bounds in the special-hyperplane case**                        | §6 Classification of Curves in P^3; pp. 349–355; L9484–9488 | If a nonsingular nonplanar curve X⊂P^3 has special hyperplane divisor, then d≥6 and g≥d/2+1.                                                                                                                                                                                               | `FC06-C04-U013`, `FC06-C04-U043`, `FC06-C04-U046`                  |
| `FC06-C04-U145` | **Theorem 6.4 — Castelnuovo bound in P^3**                                               | §6 Classification of Curves in P^3; pp. 349–355; L9490–9558 | For a nonsingular nonplanar curve of degree d and genus g in P^3: if d is even, g≤d²/4−d+1; if d is odd, g≤(d²−1)/4−d+1. Equality can hold only when the curve lies on a quadric surface.                                                                                                  | `FC06-C04-U144`, `FC06-C04-U038`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U146` | **Remark 6.4.1 — existence region for degree and genus in P^3**                          | §6 Classification of Curves in P^3; pp. 349–355; L9560–9570 | Combining the nonspecial existence theorem with Castelnuovo gives the basic allowed region for (d,g) of smooth nonplanar space curves; the remaining intermediate special cases require further constructions.                                                                             | `FC06-C04-U143`, `FC06-C04-U145`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U147` | **Example 6.4.2 — smooth space curves of degree at most seven**                          | §6 Classification of Curves in P^3; pp. 349–355; L9572–9601 | Hartshorne lists the possible genera for smooth nonplanar curves of degrees 3–7: d=3 gives g=0; d=4 gives g=0,1; d=5 gives g=0,1,2; d=6 gives g=0,1,2,3,4; d=7 gives g=0,…,6, with the extremal special cases described on quadrics or as complete intersections.                          | `FC06-C04-U146`, `FC06-C04-U145`                                   |
| `FC06-C04-U148` | **Example 6.4.3 — two families of degree-nine genus-ten curves**                         | §6 Classification of Curves in P^3; pp. 349–355; L9603–9617 | Degree-nine genus-ten smooth curves in P^3 occur in at least two distinct families: complete intersections of two cubics, and curves of type (3,6) on a smooth quadric. Their dimensions differ, illustrating reducibility phenomena in Hilbert schemes of space curves.                   | `FC06-C04-U145`, `FC06-C02-U167`                                   |

## Hartshorne, *Algebraic Geometry* — Chapter V: Surfaces

| ID              | Kind / source unit                                                                           | Location                                                 | Concise data                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    | Earlier FC06 dependencies                         |
| --------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------- |
| `FC06-C05-U001` | **Convention — surfaces, curves, and points in Chapter V**                                   | §1 Geometry on a Surface; pp. 357–368; L9649             | A surface means a nonsingular projective surface over the fixed algebraically closed field k. A curve on a surface means any effective divisor, possibly singular, reducible, or nonreduced; points are closed unless stated otherwise.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         | `FC06-C02-U273`, `FC06-C02-U091`                  |
| `FC06-C05-U002` | **Definition/construction — transverse intersection and divisor intersection pairing setup** | §1 Geometry on a Surface; pp. 357–368; L9651–9653        | Two curves C,D meet transversally at P when local equations generate the maximal ideal of O_{X,P}. Div X is the divisor group and Pic X the group of invertible sheaves, identified with divisors modulo linear equivalence.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    | `FC06-C02-U189`, `FC06-C02-U157`                  |
| `FC06-C05-U003` | **Theorem 1.1**                                                                              | §1 Geometry on a Surface; pp. 357–368; L9655             | There is a unique symmetric bilinear pairing Div X×Div X→Z, written C·D, invariant under linear equivalence and normalized so that two nonsingular curves meeting transversally have intersection equal to the number of intersection points.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   | `FC06-C02-U157`, `FC06-C02-U189`                  |
| `FC06-C05-U004` | **Lemma 1.2**                                                                                | §1 Geometry on a Surface; pp. 357–368; L9665             | Given irreducible curves C_1,…,C_r and a very ample divisor D on a surface, a general member D′∈\|D\| is irreducible, nonsingular, and meets every C_i transversally.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           | `FC06-C02-U157`, `FC06-C02-U189`, `FC06-C05-U003` |
| `FC06-C05-U005` | **Lemma 1.3**                                                                                | §1 Geometry on a Surface; pp. 357–368; L9669             | If C is an irreducible nonsingular curve and D meets C transversally, then # (C∩D)=deg_C(L(D)\|_C).                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             | `FC06-C02-U157`, `FC06-C02-U189`, `FC06-C05-U003` |
| `FC06-C05-U006` | **Proposition 1.4**                                                                          | §1 Geometry on a Surface; pp. 357–368; L9735             | If $C$ and $D$ are curves on $X$ having no common irreducible component, then $$ C . D = \sum _ { P \in C \cap D } ( C . D ) _ { P } . $$                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       | `FC06-C02-U157`, `FC06-C02-U189`, `FC06-C05-U003` |
| `FC06-C05-U007` | **Example 1.4.1**                                                                            | §1 Geometry on a Surface; pp. 357–368; L9769             | For a curve C on a surface, the self-intersection C² equals deg_C L(C)\|*C; equivalently it is the degree of the normal sheaf N*{C/X}.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          | `FC06-C02-U157`, `FC06-C02-U189`, `FC06-C05-U003` |
| `FC06-C05-U008` | **Example 1.4.2**                                                                            | §1 Geometry on a Surface; pp. 357–368; L9771             | On P² the class h of a line generates Pic P² and h²=1; hence curves of degrees n and m have intersection number nm, recovering Bézout when they have no common component.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       | `FC06-C02-U157`, `FC06-C02-U189`, `FC06-C05-U003` |
| `FC06-C05-U009` | **Example 1.4.3**                                                                            | §1 Geometry on a Surface; pp. 357–368; L9773             | On a smooth quadric Q≅P¹×P¹, the two ruling classes l,m satisfy l²=m²=0 and l·m=1; consequently divisors of types (a,b) and (a′,b′) intersect in ab′+a′b.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       | `FC06-C02-U157`, `FC06-C02-U189`, `FC06-C05-U003` |
| `FC06-C05-U010` | **Example 1.4.4**                                                                            | §1 Geometry on a Surface; pp. 357–368; L9775             | For an effective divisor D on a surface, p_a(D)=1+1/2·D·(D+K_X).                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                | `FC06-C02-U157`, `FC06-C02-U189`, `FC06-C05-U003` |
| `FC06-C05-U011` | **Proposition 1.5**                                                                          | §1 Geometry on a Surface; pp. 357–368; L9777             | Adjunction for surfaces: if C is a nonsingular genus-g curve on X and K_X is canonical, then 2g−2=C·(C+K_X).                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    | `FC06-C02-U157`, `FC06-C02-U189`, `FC06-C05-U003` |
| `FC06-C05-U012` | **Example 1.5.1**                                                                            | §1 Geometry on a Surface; pp. 357–368; L9789             | This gives a quick method of computing the genus of a curve on a surface. For example, if $C$ is a curve of degree $d$ in $\mathbf { P } ^ { 2 }$ ,then $$ 2 g \ : - \ : 2 \ : = \ : d ( d \ : - \ : 3 ) $$ so $\begin{array} { r } { \langle J \rangle = \frac { 1 } { 2 } ( d - 1 ) ( d - 2 ) } \end{array}$ Cf. (II,Ex. 8.4).                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                | `FC06-C02-U157`, `FC06-C02-U189`, `FC06-C05-U003` |
| `FC06-C05-U013` | **Example 1.5.2**                                                                            | §1 Geometry on a Surface; pp. 357–368; L9797             | If $C$ is a curve of type $^ { ( a , b ) }$ on the quadric surface, then $C + K$ has type $( \not { a } \ : - \ : 2 , \ : b \ : - \ : 2 )$ ,so $$ 2 g \ : - \ : 2 \ : = \ : a ( b \ : - \ : 2 ) \ : + \ : ( a \ : - \ : 2 ) b , $$ so $g = a b - a - b + 1$ Cf. (III,Ex. 5.6). Now we come to the Riemann-Roch theorem. For any divisor $D$ on the surface $X$ ,we let $l ( D ) = \dim _ { k } H ^ { 0 } ( X , { \mathcal { L } } ( D ) )$ . In the context of successive monoidal transformations, it is convenient to introduce the language of infinitely near points.                                                                                                                                                                                         | `FC06-C02-U239`, `FC06-C02-U243`, `FC06-C05-U064` |
| `FC06-C05-U082` | **Definition**                                                                               | §3 Monoidal Transformations; pp. 386–394; L10753         | Let $X$ be a surface. Then any point on any surface $X ^ { \prime }$ ,obtained from $X$ by a finite succession of monoidal transformations, is called an infinitely near point of $X$ If $g \colon X ^ { \prime \prime } \to X ^ { \prime }$ is a further succession of monoidal transformations,and if $Q ^ { \prime \prime } \in X ^ { \prime \prime }$ is a point in the open set where $g$ is an isomorphism, then we identify $\mathcal { Q } ^ { \prime \prime }$ with $g ( Q ^ { \prime \prime } )$ as infinitely near points of $X$ .In particular, all the ordinary points of $X$ are included among the infinitely near points.                       | `FC06-C02-U239`, `FC06-C02-U243`, `FC06-C05-U064` |
| `FC06-C05-U083` | **Example 3.9.2**                                                                            | §3 Monoidal Transformations; pp. 386–394; L10755         | Let $C$ be an irreducible curve on a surface $X$ ，with normalization $\tilde { C }$ .Then we have $$ g ( \tilde { C } ) = p _ { a } ( C ) - \sum _ { P } \frac { 1 } { 2 } r _ { P } ( r _ { P } - 1 ) , $$ where $r _ { P }$ is the multiplicity, and the sum is taken over all singular points $P$ of $C$ , including infinitely near singular points. Indeed,by (3.8) we pass from $C$ to $\tilde { C }$ by blowing up the singular points in succession,until there are none left. Each time, by (3.7), the arithmetic genus drops by ${ \frac { 1 } { 2 } } r ( r - 1 )$                                                                                   | `FC06-C02-U239`, `FC06-C02-U243`, `FC06-C05-U064` |

| `FC06-C05-U089` | **Remark 4.0.1**                                                                             | §4 The Cubic Surface in P^3; pp. 395–408; L10812         | Using this language, we can rephrase the condition (I, 7.8.2) for a complete linear system $\| D \|$ to be very ample as follows: $\| D \|$ is very ample if and only if(a) $\| D \|$ has no base points,and(b) for every $P \in X , \| D - P \|$ has no unassigned base points. | `FC06-C05-U108` | **Theorem 4.9**                                                                              | §4 The Cubic Surface in P^3; pp. 395–408; L10927         | (Twenty-Seven Lines). The cubic surface $X$ contains exactly 27 lines.Each one has self-intersection $^ { - 1 }$ ,and they are the only irreducible curves with negative self-intersection on $X$ . They are (a) the exceptional curves $E _ { i }$ $i = 1 , \ldots , 6$ (six of these), (b) the strict transform $\boldsymbol { F } _ { i j }$ of the line in $\mathbf { P } ^ { 2 }$ containing $P _ { i }$ and $P _ { j } , 1 \leqslant$ $i < j \leqslant 6$ (fifteen of these),and (c) the strict transform $G _ { j }$ of the conic in $\mathbf { P } ^ { 2 }$ containing the five $P _ { i }$ for $i \neq j , j = 1 , \ldots , 6$ (six of these).         | `FC06-C02-U220`, `FC06-C02-U239`, `FC06-C05-U087` |
| `FC06-C05-U109` | **Remark 4.9.1**                                                                             | §4 The Cubic Surface in P^3; pp. 395–408; L10963         | The twelve lines E_1,…,E_6,G_1,…,G_6 form a Schläfli double-six: each family is pairwise skew and E_i meets G_j exactly for i≠j. Such a double-six determines the classical configuration of 27 lines on a smooth cubic surface.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                | `FC06-C02-U220`, `FC06-C02-U239`, `FC06-C05-U087` |
| `FC06-C05-U110` | **Proposition 4.10**                                                                         | §4 The Cubic Surface in P^3; pp. 395–408; L10965         | Let $X$ be a cubic surface as abore,and let $E _ { 1 } ^ { ' } , \dots , E _ { 6 } ^ { ' }$ he any subset of six mutually skew lines chosen from among the 27 lines on $X$ .Then there is another morphism $\pi ^ { \prime } : X \to \mathbf { P } ^ { 2 }$ ,making $X$ isomorphic to that $\mathbf { P } ^ { 2 }$ with six points $P _ { 1 } ^ { \prime } , \ldots , P _ { 6 } ^ { \prime }$ blown up (no 3 collinear and not all 6 on a conic),such that $E _ { 1 } ^ { \prime } , \ldots , E _ { 6 } ^ { \prime }$ are the exceptional curves for $\pi ^ { \prime }$                                                                                         | `FC06-C02-U220`, `FC06-C02-U239`, `FC06-C05-U087` |

| `FC06-C05-U117` | **Definition/construction — graph and total transform of a birational transformation**       | §5 Birational Transformations; pp. 409–420; L11113       | The graph Γ_T is the closure in X×Y of the graph over the domain of definition. | `FC06-C05-U118` | **Lemma 5.1**                                                                                | §5 Birational Transformations; pp. 409–420; L11115       | For a birational transformation T:X⇢Y of projective varieties with X normal, the fundamental locus is closed of codimension at least two.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       | `FC06-C01-U072`, `FC06-C02-U091`, `FC06-C05-U116` |
| `FC06-C05-U119` | **Example 5.1.1**                                                                            | §5 Birational Transformations; pp. 409–420; L11119       | Let $X$ be a surface,and let $\pi { \colon } \tilde { X } \to X$ be the monoidal transformation with center $P$ Then $\pi$ is defined everywhere. Its inverse $\pi ^ { - 1 } : X \to { \tilde { X } }$ is a birational transformation having $P$ as a fundamental point.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        | `FC06-C01-U072`, `FC06-C02-U091`, `FC06-C05-U116` |
| `FC06-C05-U120` | **Theorem 5.2**                                                                              | §5 Birational Transformations; pp. 409–420; L11121       | Zariski Main Theorem in this form: if P is a fundamental point of a birational transformation from normal X, its total transform is connected; in the surface case it contains a curve.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         | `FC06-C01-U072`, `FC06-C02-U091`, `FC06-C05-U116` |
| `FC06-C05-U121` | **Proposition 5.3**                                                                          | §5 Birational Transformations; pp. 409–420; L11129       | If f:X′→X is a birational morphism of nonsingular surfaces and P is a fundamental point of f^{-1}, then f factors through the blowup of X at P.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 | `FC06-C01-U072`, `FC06-C02-U091`, `FC06-C05-U116` |
| `FC06-C05-U122` | **Corollary 5.4**                                                                            | §5 Birational Transformations; pp. 409–420; L11143       | A birational morphism of nonsingular projective surfaces factors into finitely many monoidal transformations; the number equals the number of irreducible curves contracted to points.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          | `FC06-C01-U072`, `FC06-C02-U091`, `FC06-C05-U116` |
| `FC06-C05-U123` | **Remark 5.4.1**                                                                             | §5 Birational Transformations; pp. 409–420; L11151       | It is interesting to compare the factorization of (5.3) with the universal property of blowing up proved in (II, 7.14). While the new result implies the old one in the special case of blowing up a point (since $f ^ { - 1 } \mathfrak { m } _ { P } \cdot \mathcal { O } _ { X } ,$ being invertible implies $f ^ { - 1 } ( P )$ has dimension 1,so $P$ is a fundamental point),it is actually stronger, since it uses Zariski's Main Theorem. We cannot deduce (5.3) from (II, 7.14), because the hypothesis that $f ^ { - 1 } \mathfrak { m } _ { P } \cdot \ell _ { X ^ { \prime } } ^ { \prime }$ is invertible is impossible to verify in our case.     | `FC06-C01-U072`, `FC06-C02-U091`, `FC06-C05-U116` |
| `FC06-C05-U124` | **Remark 5.4.2**                                                                             | §5 Birational Transformations; pp. 409–420; L11153       | Comparing (5.4) to the earlier theorem (II, 7.17), we see that the new result is more precise, because it uses only monoidal transformations,rather than the more general concept of blowing up an arbitrary sheaf of ideals.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   | `FC06-C01-U072`, `FC06-C02-U091`, `FC06-C05-U116` |
| `FC06-C05-U125` | **Remark 5.4.3**                                                                             | §5 Birational Transformations; pp. 409–420; L11155       | The surface factorization statement fails in dimension at least three: blowing up a nonsingular curve in a smooth threefold cannot in general be factored first through the blowup of an arbitrary point of that curve.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         | `FC06-C01-U072`, `FC06-C02-U091`, `FC06-C05-U116` |
| `FC06-C05-U126` | **Remark 5.4.4**                                                                             | §5 Birational Transformations; pp. 409–420; L11157       | Even allowing monoidal transformations along nonsingular centers, an arbitrary birational morphism of smooth projective varieties need not factor this way in dimensions at least three.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        | `FC06-C01-U072`, `FC06-C02-U091`, `FC06-C05-U116` |
| `FC06-C05-U127` | **Theorem 5.5**                                                                              | §5 Birational Transformations; pp. 409–420; L11159       | Every birational transformation between nonsingular projective surfaces factors into a finite sequence of monoidal transformations and their inverses.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          | `FC06-C01-U072`, `FC06-C02-U091`, `FC06-C05-U116` |

| `FC06-C05-U129` | **Remark 5.6.1**                                                                             | §5 Birational Transformations; pp. 409–420; L11177       | In characteristic zero, Hironaka resolution supplies a common resolution of a birational map by successive blowups along nonsingular centers; Hartshorne notes this yields birational invariance of the arithmetic genus in all dimensions.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     | `FC06-C01-U072`, `FC06-C02-U091`, `FC06-C05-U116` |
| `FC06-C05-U130` | **Theorem 5.7**                                                                              | §5 Birational Transformations; pp. 409–420; L11181       | Castelnuovo criterion: any exceptional curve E≅P^1 with E^2=−1 can be contracted to a nonsingular point; equivalently the surface is the blowup of the contracted surface at that point.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        | `FC06-C01-U072`, `FC06-C02-U091`, `FC06-C05-U116` |

| `FC06-C05-U132` | **Remark 5.7.2**                                                                             | §5 Birational Transformations; pp. 409–420; L11266       | Hartshorne formulates the general contraction problem: a closed subset Y⊂X is contractible if a birational morphism contracts exactly Y to one point and is an isomorphism off Y.
